7 .3.5 The  Committee  notes  that  the  Autonomous  Bodies  under  the  Department
of  Sports — namely  the  Sports  Authority  of  India  (SAI),  National  Anti-Doping
Agency  (NADA),  National  Dope  Testing  Laboratory  (NDTL),  National  Sports
University  (NSU)  and  Lakshmibai  National  Institute  of  Physical  Education
(LNIPE) — play  a  pivotal  role  in  strengthening  training  infrastructure,  anti-
doping  mechanisms,  sports  education  and  scientific  support  systems.  While  the
overall  allocation  under  Other  Central  Sector  Expenditure  –  Autonomous  Bodies
has  increased  from  ₹ 1,603.73  crore  at  RE  2025 – 26  to  ₹ 1,777.90  crore  at  BE
2026 – 27,  there  is  a  considerable  variations  and  fluctuations  in  fund  allocations

70
between  Autonomous  Bodies  over  the  years.  The  Committee  recommends  that  a
performance-based  funding  framework  may  be  adopted  for  Autonomous  Bodies,
with  a  focus  to  measurable  outcomes  such  as  medal  performance,  research
output, anti-doping compliance standards  and  athlete outreach  parameters .
7 .3. 6 The  Committee  observes  variations  in  fund  allocations  to  a  few  institutions,
particularly  the  National  Sports  University  and  the  National  Dope  Testing
Laboratory,  with  significant  differences  between  Budget  Estimates  and  Revised
Estimates  in  successive  years.  Such  variations  may  affect  planning,  faculty
recruitment,  research  continuity  and  laboratory  accreditation  standards.  The
Committee,  therefore,  recommends  that  annual  budgetary  provisions  be  aligned
with  approved  long-term  development  plans  of  these  institutions,  with  funds
provisions  for  committed  liabilities  and  capital  projects,  so  as  to  ensure  stability
of such institutions .
7 .3.7 The  Committee  also  notes  the  central  role  of  the  Sports  Authority  of  India,
which  continues  to  receive  the  largest  share  of  funding  among  Autonomous
Bodies,  alongside  critical  institutions  such  as  NADA ,  NDTL  etc.  In  view  of  the
increasing  emphasis  on  clean  and  scientific  training  of  sports ,  the  Committee
recommends  that  greater  institutional  coordination  among  SAI,  NADA,  NDTL,
NSU  and  LNIPE  should  be  developed  through  a  inter-institutional  coordination
mechanism  for  data  sharing,  research  collaboration,  sports  science  integration
and  athlete  monitoring,  thereby  optimizing  resource  utilization  and  enhancing
sports  performance ecosystem  of the country .
7 .2  SPORT S  AUTHORITY OF INDIA (SAI) :
7 .2 .1  The objectives of the Sports Authority of India (SAI) are as follows:
 Promote sports in the country
 Achieve sporting excellence at the national and international level
 Implement  schemes/programmes  for  achieving  excellence  in  sports  in  different
disciplines  at  international  level  in  order  to  establish  India  as  a  major  sporting
power
 Provide international exposure and training
 Prepare and train national teams
 Maintain and develop sports infrastructure
 Improve and monitor performance with a scientific evaluation procedure
 Scout talent at a micro level and nurture talent towards excellence
 Support training with sports and scientific equipment and scientific personnel.
7 . 2.2  Sports  Authority  of  India  (SAI)  is  implementing  the  following  sports
promotional  schemes  across  the  country  to  identify  talented  sports  persons  in  various
age groups and nurture them to excel at national and international competitions:
Table-3 1
SN Scheme(s)
1 National Centres of Excellence (NCOE)

71
2 SAI Training Centre (STC)
3 Extension Centre of STC
4 National Sports Talent Contest (NSTC)
(i) Indigenous Games & Martial Arts Schools (IGMA)
(ii) Akharas
The  Schemes  enable  talent  identification  and  nurturing  of  identified  talent  as  per
details given below:
SAI National Centres of Excellence (NCOE)
7 .2.3  The  Promising  athletes  are  provided  state  of  the  art  infrastructure  and  playing  facilities,
sports  science  backup,  individualized  diet  prescribed  by  trained  nutritionists  and  overall  supervision
under  the  best  coaches,  qualified  support  staff  and  High  Performance  Directors.  The  athletes  are
provided  with  Lodging,  Boarding,  Sports  Kit,  Competition  Exposure,  Education  Expense,  and
Miscellaneous  expenses.  National  Centres  of  Excellence  (26  Centres)  also  operate s  as  regular
coaching camps for the best available talent among juniors in India.
SAI Training Centres (STC)
7 .2.4  In  order  to  groom  the  junior  level  sports  persons  in  the  age  group  of  10-18  years,  SAI
Training  Centres  (STC)  (69  Centres)  are  established  in  a  State  where  the  sports  infrastructure  is
provided  by  the  respective  State  Government  where  regular  training  is  being  imparted  in  27  sports
discipline.  Trainees  under  the  residential  scheme  with  Boarding,  Sports  Kit,  Competition  Exposure,
Educational  Expenses,  Medical  Expenses  and  Insurance  and  under  Non-Residential  Scheme  are
provided Sports Kit, Competition Exposure, Insurance and Stipend  are provided .
Extension Centres of STC
7 .2.5  With  a  view  to  develop  sports  standards  in  schools  and  colleges  having  requisite  sports
infrastructure  in  a  given  sports , the  Extension  Centres  Scheme  (22  Centres)  was  launched.  The
children  in  the  age  group  of  10-18  years  are  admitted  in  the  scheme  on  Non-Residential  basis.  The
trainees  admitted  under  the  scheme  are  provided  with  Stipend  ofRs.6000/-  per  month  per  head  for
10  months  in  a  year,  Competition  Exposure  - Rs.3000/-,  Sports  Kit  - Rs.5000/-,  Insurance  - Rs.150/-
per  head  per  annum  and  infrastructure  and  equipment  support  to  the  identified  institution  to  the  tune
of Rs.5000/- per trainee per annum subject to ceiling of Rs.1.00 lakh .
National Sports Talent Contest Scheme (NSTC)
7 .2.6  National  Sports  Talent  Contest  (NSTC)  Scheme  64  Centres  is  being  implemented  to
scout  sports  talent  in  the  age  group  of  8-14  years  from  schools  and  nurture  them  into  future  medal
hopes  by  providing  scientific  training.  The  main  concept  of  the  scheme  is  to  provide  support  to
athletes of 8-14 years.
(i)  Indigenous  Games  &  Martial  Arts :India  is  an  ancient  country  with  a  rich  cultural
heritage  as  there  are  many  traditional  games  popular  in  the  country.  Practitioners  of  these
games  are  on  the  decline  and  need  was  felt  for  their  revival.  Under  the  IGMA  scheme , SAI
has  adopted  09  Schools  and  the  trainees  under  the  scheme  are  provided  with  Stipend
@Rs.3000/-  per  head  per  month  for  10  months,  Sports  Kit  of  @Rs.1500/-,  Insurance
@Rs.150/- per head per annum.

72
(ii)  Adoption  of  Akharas  under  NSTC  scheme:  Efforts  are  being  made  to  create  a  broader
base  for  modern  wrestling  and  supplement  the  efforts  made  by  various  Akharas  in  the
country.  SAI  has  adopted  50  Akharas  and  the  adopted  Akharas  who  are  provided  with  one
set  of  Wrestling  Mat  and  Multi-Gym.  The  trainees  in  the  Akharas  are  provided  a  Stipend  of
Rs.1000/-  per  head  per  month  for  10  months,  Sports  Kits  - Rs.3000/-,  Competition  Exposure
- Rs.3000/- and Insurance of Rs.150/- per head per annum.
7 .2.7 An  allocation  of  Rs.  ₹ 917.38  crore  has  been  made  at  BE  (2026 – 27)  for  “ Sports  Authority  of
India ”  while BE (2025-26) was Rs. 830.00 crore which was increased to Rs. 880 Cr at the RE stage.
7 .2.8 The  Committee  was  informed  that  the  increase  in  Budget  Estimate  (BE)  2026-27  allocations
for  Sports  Authority  of  India  (SAI)  is  due  to  various  reasons  including  appointment  of  regular
officers/employees,  contractual  staff,  court  case  arrears,  retirement  benefits  and  increase  in  DA  etc.
Further,  it  was  also  informed  that  the  enhanced  funds  will  be  utilized  towards  greater  requirement
for  international  training/  exposure,  strengthening  of  National  Centres  of  Excellence  (NCoEs ),
expanded  athlete  base  under  Target  Olympic  Podium  scheme  (TOPS)  and  upgradation  of  sports
science and medical facilities.
7 .2.9 Besides,  the  Department  of  Sports,  Government  of  India,  through  the  Sports  Authority  of
India  (SAI),  ensures  systematic  grooming  of  prospective  and  potential  sportspersons  through  a
structured  and  multi-tiered  talent  identification  and  development  framework.  Talent  scouting  is
conducted  at  the  grassroots  level  through  district  and  State  competitions,  school  and  university
championships,  and  in  collaboration  with  National  Sports  Federations  and  State  Sports  Bodies.
Under  the  Khelo  India  Scheme,  the  identification  of  Khelo  India  Athletes  (KIAs)  is  carried  out  in
accordance  with  laid-down  protocols  by  a  specialized  Talent  Identification  and  Development
Committee  (TIDC)  comprising  Olympians,  eminent  athletes  and  experienced  coaches,  who  scout
talent  during  competitions,  recommend  athletes  for  assessment  camps,  and  finalize  selections  based
on  performance  benchmarks  and  vacancies.  Identified  athletes  are  placed  on  structured  long-term
development  pathways  covering  technical  coaching,  physical  conditioning,  psychological  support,
nutrition  guidance  and  competition  exposure,  with  periodic  performance  monitoring  by
multidisciplinary  teams.  At  present,  1,067  Khelo  India  Centres  have  been  notified  across  36
States/UTs  training  28,214  athletes,  35  Khelo  India  State  Centres  of  Excellence  are  supporting
2,464  athletes,  and  2,905  KIAs  across  22  sports  disciplines  are  receiving  comprehensive  assistance
of  ₹ 6.28  lakh  per  annum,  including  a  scholarship  of  ₹ 10,000  per  month  as  out-of-pocket  allowance,
access  to  sports  science  support  and  state-of-the-art  training  facilities,  including  322  accredited
academies.
7 .2.10 Further,  structured  grooming  is  reinforced  through  identification  under  the  Target  Olympic
Podium  Scheme  (TOPS)  Development  Group,  Monitoring  of  Champions  (MOC)  reviews,  adoption
of  the  Long-Term  Athlete  Development  (LTAD)  framework,  and  integration  of  Khelo  India  athletes
into  elite  performance.  This  mechanism  ensures  transparent  tracking  of  funding  extended  to
budding  sportspersons  during  the  current  financial  year  and  facilitates  data-driven  decisions  for
their continued preparation for forthcoming national and international sporting events.
7 .2.11 The  Sports  Authority  of  India  (SAI)  has  also  issued  a  circular  regarding  the  safety  of  women
in  the  National  Coaching  Camps  and  during  international  exposures.  Further,  to  ensure  that  athletes

73
have  a  safe  environment  to  live,  study  and  training,  an  autonomous  body  under  the  aegis  of  Govt.  of
India  through  Sports  Authority  of  India  (SAI)  implement  a  strong  system  in  place  to  prevent  and
redress cases of sexual harassment, which are under as:
i. There  is  an  Internal  Complaint  Committee  (ICC)  constituted  at  all  Regional  Centers  and
academic  institutions  of  SAI  with  a  senior  lady  officer  as  the  Chairperson  of  the
Committee.  Members  of  the  Committee  also  include  a  lady  official  from  a  local  NGO
and lady officers of SAI.
ii. To  sensitize  athletes  about  issues  of  sexual  harassment  and  to  strengthen  them  to  come
forward  and  register  complaints,  workshops  in  local  languages  are  conducted  in  every
regional center on a regular basis. 
iii. A  toll-free  call  center  is  also  functioning  24X7  to  aid  the  trainees  to  register  complaints
regarding sexual harassment.
iv. Sports  Hostels  under  SAI  are  equipped  with  comprehensive  safety  and  security
arrangements, including:
a. Adequate lighting in hostels and surrounding areas
b. CCTV surveillance in common areas;
c. Restricted entry and exit protocols;
d. Deployment of female wardens and caretakers;
e. 24x7 security personnel;
f. Visitor management systems
7 .2.12 Apart  from  above,  all  the  organizations  under  the  Department  have  functional  ICC  as  per  the
statutory requirement.
7 .2.13 There  is  a  separate  Cadre  of  Coaches  in  the  Sports  Authority  of  India  (SAI).  The
Recruitment  is  carried  out  as  per  Recruitment  Rules  of  SAI  and  is  a  continuous  process.  Further,  the

7 .2.18 The  Committee  notes  that  the  Sports  Authority  of  India  (SAI)  continues  to  play  a
central  role  in  achieving  sporting  excellence  through  its  multi-tiered  talent  identification  and
training  framework  comprising  National  Centres  of  Excellence  (NCOEs),  SAI  Training
Centres  (STCs),  Extension  Centres  and  NSTC  schemes.  In  view  of  the  enhanced  allocation  of
₹ 917.38  crore  in  BE  2026 – 27  as  compared  to  ₹ 880  crore  at  RE  2025 – 26,  the  Committee
recommends  that  SAI  should  formulate  performance  based  such  as  medal  conversion  rates,
athlete  progression  from  STCs  to  NCOEs,  and  other  performance  benchmarks  to  financial
allocations , so as to ensure optimal utilization of enhanced budgetary support.

78
7 .2.19 The  Committee  observes  that  while  SAI  has  expanded  its  footprint  across  10  Regional
Centres  and  2  Academic  Institutions  with  187  operational  units  nationwide,  disparities  in
infrastructure  development  and  utilization  and  athlete  density  across  regions  remain  as
bottlenecks  for  balanced  sports  development.  The  Committee  recommends  that  a  region-wise
capacity  and  performance  audit  be  undertaken  to  rationalize  disciplines  based  on  local
strengths,  optimize  coach  deployment,  and  ensure  equitable  access  to  high-quality  training
facilities, particularly in tribal, rural and remote areas.
7 .2.20 The  Committee  appreciates  the  structured  talent  scouting  mechanism  under  Khelo
India,  TOPS  Development  Group  and  Long-Term  Athlete  Development  (LTAD)  framework,
supported  by  scientific  monitoring  and  multidisciplinary  teams.  However,  to  strengthen
transparency  and  long-term  athlete  tracking,  the  Committee  recommends  development  of  an
integrated  digital  athlete  performance  management  system  across  all  SAI  schemes,  taking
into  account  data  on  training  load,  injury  management,  competition  results  etc. , thereby
enabling practical  decision-making for overall development of Sports in the country .
7 .2. 21 The  Committee  takes  note  of  the  recruitment  of  coaches  under  regular  and  contractual
categories  and  recognizes  the  importance  of  qualified  human  resources  in  achiev ing  high
performance  standards.  T he  Committee , therefore,  recommends  to  e x ped ite  the  filling  up  of
sanctioned  vacancies  through  a  time-bound  recruitment  calendar,  thereby  enhancing
continuous  professional  development  of  coaches  through  international  certifications  and
exposure .
7 .2.2 2 The  Committee  acknowledges  the  measures  adopted  by  SAI  to  ensure  safety  and
welfare  of  athletes,  including  constitution  of  Internal  Complaints  Committees,  24×7  toll-free
helplines  and  hostel  security  arrangements.  While  these  initiatives  are  commendable,  the
Committee  is  of  the  view  that  periodic  independent  safety  audits  of  SAI  centres , mandatory
gender-sensitization  and  safeguarding  workshops  for  all  staff  and  trainees,  should  be
considered , so as to create  a secure and sporting environment across all SAI establishments.
7.3 LAKSHMIBAI NATIONAL INSTITUTE OF PHYSICAL ED UCATION GWALIOR
7 .3.1 The Committee was informed  about the objective of the Institute i .e., to :
i. P rovide  higher  education  leading  to  excellence  and  innovation  in  such  branches  of
knowledge as may be deemed fit primarily at post-graduate and research degree levels ;
ii. To  engage  in  areas  of  specialization  with  proven  ability  to  make  distinctive  contributions  to
the  objectives  of  the  university  education  system  that  is  –  academic  engagement  clearly
distinguishable  from  programs  of  an  ordinary  nature  that  lead  to  conventional  degrees  in
arts,  science  engineering,  medicine,  dental,  pharmacy,  management,  etc.  routinely  offered
by con ventional institutions; and
iii. To  provide  high  quality  teaching  and  research  for  the  advancement,  knowledge  and  its
dissemination through various research programs undertaken.
7.3.2 The  Committee  was  informed  that  u nder  the  Head  “ Laxmi  Bai  National  Institute  of  Physical
Education ” , an  increased  allocation  of  Rs.  87.50  crore  has  been  made  at  BE  2026-27  vis-à-vis  RE
2025-26  i.e.  Rs.79.96  crore.  It  was  also  informed  that  the  increased  allocation  of  ₹  87.50  crore  has
been  made  due  to  the  annual  increment  and  revision  of  DA  during  2026-27  of  Basic  Salary  and

79
inflation.  Similarly,  the  pension  and  family  pension  expenditure  is  also  likely  to  be  enhanced  by  6-
7%  of  Basic  pension.  Some  posts  of  academic  &  administrative  staff  are  also  likely  to  be  filled  up
during  2026-27.  Further,  construction  in  both  campuses  of  LNIPE  (in  Gwalior  and  NERC)  is
expected in 2026-27.
7.3.3 The  Committee  notes  that  the  Lakshmibai  National  Institute  of  Physical  Education
(LNIPE),  Gwalior,  is  dedicated  to  higher  education,  research  and  specialization  in  physical
education  and  sports  sciences  and  plays  a  crucial  role  in  strengthening  the  academic  and
professional  foundation  of  the  sports  ecosystem  in  the  country.  While  the  enhanced  allocation
of  ₹ 87.50  crore  in  BE  2026 – 27  over  ₹ 79.96  crore  at  RE  2025 – 26  is  primarily  attributable  to
salary  revisions,  pension  liabilities,  filling  up  of  posts  and  anticipated  construction  activities  at
both  Gwalior  and  NERC  campuses,  the  Committee  is  of  the  view  that  a  comprehensive
academic  and  infrastructure  development  plan  should  be  prepared  with  clearly  defined
research  priorities,  faculty  recruitment  plans  etc.  so  as  to  ensure  that  higher  allocations
translates  into  tangible  improvements  in  teaching  quality,  research  output  and  institutional
excellence in physical education and sports sciences.
7 .4 FIT INDIA
7 .4.1 Fit  India  Movement  was  launched  on  29th  August,  2019  by  Hon ’ble  Prime  Minister  with  a
vision  of  “ Hum  Fit  Toh  India  Fit ”  and  with  the  slogan  “ Fitness  ki  Dose,  Aadha  Ghanta  Roz ” . The
mission  of  the  Movement  is  to  bring  about  behavioural  changes  and  move  towards  a  more
physically  active  lifestyle.  Towards  achieving  this  mission,  Fit  India  proposes  to  undertake  various
initiatives and conduct events to achieve the following objectives; 
a. To promote fitness as easy, fun and free
b.  To  spread  awareness  on  fitness  and  various  physical  activities  that  promote  fitness
through focused campaigns
c. To encourage indigenous sports
d. To make fitness reach every school, college/university, panchayat /village, etc.
e.  To  create  a  platform  for  citizens  of  India  to  share  information,  drive  awareness  and
encourage sharing of personal fitness stories
f. To achieve policy convergence to promote the overall vision of the movement.
7 .4.2 In  pursuit  of  the  above  objectives,  the  Fit  India  Mission  has  undertaken  various  initiatives
and activities as under -
Sundays  on  Cycle  (SoC ),  a  Flagship  Fit  India  Initiative:  Sundays  on  Cycle  has  emerged  as  one
of  the  most  impactful  and  recognisable  initiatives  under  the  Fit  India  Movement.  T he  initiative  has
rapidly  grown  and  expanded  to  over  2.2  lakh  locations,  engaging  more  than  25  lakh  participants
across  cities,  towns,  campuses,  industrial  areas,  border  regions  and  rural  pockets.  While  cycling
remains  the  core  activity,  editions  now  routinely  integrate  Yoga,  Zumba , rope  skipping,  functional
fitness  and  indigenous  physical  activities,  making  the  platform  inclusive  for  all  age  groups,  fitness
levels and local contexts. 

80
National  Sports  Day:  National  Sports  Day  was  observed  from  29-31  August  2025,  engaging
nearly  30  crore  citizens  across  700  districts  and  10,000+  locations  through  sports  activities,  fitness
pledges,  indigenous  games,  and  cycling  rallies.  The  campaign  generated  over  1.70  billion  digital
media impressions, significantly amplifying nationwide outreach.
International  Yoga  Day:  The  11 th International  Day  of  Yoga  was  celebrated  across  450+  locations,
mobilising  over  50,000  participants  nationwide.  Events  were  organised  at  23  NCOEs,  69  STCs  and
150+  Khelo  India  Centres,  with  participation  of  25,500+  individuals  from  NCOEs/STCs  and
12,000+ from KICs/KISCEs.
Fit  India  Collaborations:  Under  Fit  India ’s  collaborative  initiatives,  150  cyclists  completed  the
4,049  km  Kashmir  to  Kanyakumari  Ride  for  Unity  in  17  days  and  16  nights,  symbolising  national
integration  and  the  spirit  of  Ek  Bharat,  Shreshtha  Bharat.  Additionally,  the  Pedal  to  Plant  –  Naya
Bharat,  Hara  Bharat  Yatra , a  4,000  km  cycling  expedition  from  Arunachal  Pradesh  to  Gujarat,  was
flagged  off  on  1  October  2025  from  Pangsau  Pass,  Changlang  District,  promoting  environmental
sustainability alongside national unity.
The  Fit  India  Carnival:  Several  Fit  India  Carnivals  were  organised  across  the  country  a
community-centric  outreach  initiative  designed  to  promote  fitness  through  interactive  activities,
demonstrations,  and  engagement  zones.  The  Carnival  format  attracted  families,  youth,  and  first-
time  participants,  enhancing  public  visibility  of  Fit  India  initiatives  and  encouraging  wider
community participation in physical activity.
Fit  India  Freedom  Run:  The  Fit  India  Freedom  Run,  launched  in  2020,  encourages  people  to
dedicate  at  least  30  minutes  daily  to  physical  activity,  while  spreading  the  dual  message  of
Swachhata  +  Swasthata  (Cleanliness  +  Health).  In  its  6th  edition,  held  from  2nd  October  (Gandhi
Jayanti ) to  31st  October  (National  Unity  Day),  crores  of  citizens  across  the  country  joined  the
impactful run. 
Fit  India  Mobile  App:  The  Fit  India  Mobile  App  recorded  over  13  lakh  downloads  and  is  available
in  12  languages.  The  app  saw  milestone  achievements  through  the  launch  of  ‘Carbon  Credit ’ and
‘Leaderboard .’
Fit  India  Zone:  Fit  India  Zones  were  established  during  the  Khelo  India  University  Games  2025  in
Jaipur  (25  November-5  December)  and  subsequently  at  Porbandar  on  14  December  2025,  attracting
large public participation and significantly enhancing awareness of Fit India initiatives.
NaMo  Fit  India  Cycling  Clubs :NaMo  Fit  India  Cycling  Clubs  provide  a  structured  community
platform for cycling, running, walking and yoga, fostering regular participation and habit formation.
The  Fit  India  School  Certification  Program  is  currently  being  revised,  mainly  focusing  on  physical
education in school curriculum and training of physical instructors/trainers/coaches .
7 .4.3 The  Committee  notes  with  appreciation  that  the  Fit  India  Movement  has  evolved  into  a
nationwide  behavioural  transformation  initiative,  with  extensive  outreach  through
programmes  such  as  Sundays  on  Cycle,  Fit  India  Freedom  Run,  National  Sports  Day
celebrations,  International  Yoga  Day  activities,  Fit  India  Carnivals,  Cycling  Clubs  and  the  Fit

81
India  Mobile  App.  The  large-scale  participation  across  districts,  educational  institutions  and
rural  areas  reflects  commendable  public  engagement  and  policy  convergence.  However,  in
order  to  ensure  sustained  impact  beyond  event-based  mobilization,  the  Committee
recommends  that  a  systematic  monitoring  and  evaluation  framework  with  outcome  indicators
such  as  measurable  improvements  in  physical  activity  levels  in  schools  and  communities,
integration  of  physical  education  in  curricula,  utilisation  metrics  of  Fit  India  Zones  and
Cycling  Clubs,  and  behavioral  change  surveys  may  be  developed  so  that  the  Movement
transitions  from  mass  participation  campaigns  to  a  fitness  ecosystem  with  long-term  public
health and youth development goals.
7 .5  NATIONAL DOPE TESTING LABORATORY
7 .5.1 In  order  to  improve  the  performance  of  National  Dope  Testing  Laboratory  (NDTL)  and  keep
the  laboratory  upgraded  as  per  the  latest  guidelines  as  enshrined  under  World  Anti-Doping  Agency
(WADA)  International  Standards  for  Laboratories  (ISL)  and  National  Accreditation  Board  for
Testing  and  Calibration  Laboratories  (NABL) . The  laboratory  is  taking  all  the  necessary  measures
to  ensure  implementation  of  the  latest  WADA  guidelines.  WADA  evaluates  the  laboratory
performance  every  year  through  the  analysis  of  control  samples  in  terms  of  proficiency  testing
conducted  by  WADA  anti-doping  laboratories  worldwide  as  part  of  the  WADA  External  Quality
Assessment  Scheme  (EQAS)  of  urine  samples  and  blood  samples.  The  laboratory  ensures  that  all
the  guidelines  of  WADA  are  properly  implemented  through  the  Quality  Assurance  System.
Further ,the  laboratory  has  already  received  accreditation  for  the  year  2026.  NABL  accreditation  has
also  been  granted  to  the  testing  laboratory  as  per  the  ISO  17025:2017  laboratory  management
system,  and  desktop  surveillance  is  conducted  every  year.  NDTL  has  renewed  the  NABL
accreditation  under  ISO  17025  for  the  period  from  23.04.2024  to  22.04.2026  (two  years),  based  on
the performance of the laboratory management system audit conducted by NABL.
7 .5. 2 The  Laboratory  has  established  an  Athlete  Passport  Management  Unit  (APMU)  in  India.
WADA  has  approved  the  National  Dope  Testing  Laboratory  (NDTL),  New  Delhi,  to  manage  the
Athlete  Biological  Passport  (ABP)  as  a  WADA-approved  APMU.  This  approval  marks  a  significant
milestone  for  India ’s  anti-doping  initiatives,  making  India  the  17th  country  worldwide  with  a
WADA-approved  APMU.  The  India  APMU  will  serve  the  country  as  well  as  anti-doping
organizations, including those in neighbouring  countries.
7 .5. 3 The  laboratory  has  taken  the  following  steps  to  keep  the  Laboratory  upgraded  as  per  the
latest guidelines of the WADA and NABL:
 Strengthened Quality Management System.
 Updating  technical  methods  for  initial  testing  &  confirmation  procedures  as  per  WADA
ISL-2021.
 Implementation  of  improved  research  programmes  including  MoUs  with  National  Institute
of  repute  such  as  National  Institute  of  Pharmaceutical  Education  and  Research  (NIPER),
Guwahati  and  CSIR-IIIM,  Jammu,  Delhi  Pharmaceuticals  Sciences  and  Research
University (DPSRU) and National Forensic Sciences University (NFSU), Gujarat.
 Collaborated  with  three  WADA-accredited  laboratories  for  undertaking  international
collaborations and research in niche areas of dope testing.

82
 Scheduled  training  programmes  by  International  Experts  drawn  from  WADA-  accredited
Laboratories for imparting training to the Scientists and other staff.
 Strengthening the physical resources.
 Upgradation  of  Civil,  Electrical  and  IT  infrastructure  of  the  Laboratory  as  per  WADA
ISLs.
7 .5. 4 The revenues generated by NDTL during the last five years are as under:
Table-3 7

2022-23 6.79

2024-25 0.96*

*  The  Governing  Body  of  the  National  Dope  Testing  Laboratory  (NDTL)  has  waived  the  Dope
Control  Testing  Fee  for  NADA  with  effect  from  the  year  2024 – 25.  As  the  majority  of  Dope  Control
Testing  samples  is  being  received  by  NDTL  from  the  National  Anti-Doping  Agency  (NADA),  this
waiver has resulted in a reduction in internal income of NDTL from 2024 – 25 onwards.
7 .5. 5 The  Committee  notes  with  satisfaction  that  the  National  Dope  Testing  Laboratory
(NDTL)  has  secured  continued  accreditation  from  WADA  and  NABL  under  ISO  17025:2017,
established  a  WADA-approved  Athlete  Passport  Management  Unit,  and  strengthened  its
Quality  Management  Systems,  research  collaborations  and  infrastructure  in  accordance  with
international  standards.  These  developments  signify  India ’s  growing  credibility  in  the  global
anti-doping  framework.  However,  considering  the  dynamic  nature  of  doping  methodologies
and  the  need  for  constant  technological  upgradation , the  Committee  recommends  that  the
Ministry  should  ensure  assured  and  dedicated  financial  support  for  periodic  modernization  of
analytical  equipment,  expansion  of  research  capabilities,  and  continuous  capacity  building  of
scientific  personnel,  so  that  NDTL  remains  at  par  with  leading  WADA-accredited
laboratories worldwide.
7 .5.6 The  Committee  notes  a  sharp  decline  in  internal  revenue  generation  from  2024 – 25
onwards  due  to  the  waiver  of  Dope  Control  Testing  Fee  for  NADA,  which  constitutes  the
majority  of  sample  inflow.  While  the  waiver  may  serve  broader  policy  objectives,  the
resultant  reduction  in  institutional  revenue  generation  could  impact  long-term  operational
sustainability  and  research  activities.  The  Committee,  therefore,  recommends  that  a
calibrated  funding  mechanism — either  through  enhanced  budgetary  support  or
diversification  of  testing  services  including  regional  collaborations  and  specialised  research
assignments  should  be  developed  to  compensate  for  the  revenue  shortfall  and  ensure  that  the
L aboratory ’s financial stability is not compromised.
7 .6  NATIONAL ANTI-DOPING AGENCY

83
7 .6.1 The  National  Anti-Doping  Agency  (NADA)  is  the  apex  body  in  India  responsible  for
promoting,  coordinating,  and  monitoring  the  doping  control  program  in  sports.  Its  mandate  is  to
ensure  that  all  sports  activities  in  the  country  are  free  from  doping  practices,  aligning  with  the
international  code  established  by  the  World  Anti-Doping  Agency  (WADA) . The  p rimary  functions
of NADA  are given below:
i. Doping  Control : Conduct  in-competition  and  out-of-competition  testing  of  athletes.  Collect
and analyze samples in accredited laboratories.
ii. Results  Management : Handle  the  process  of  identifying  violations  and  determining
sanctions. Ensure compliance with international standards for testing and results.
iii. Anti-Doping  Education  Awareness : Organi s e  workshops,  seminars,  and  campaigns  to
spread  awareness  about  anti-doping  rules  and  the  dangers  of  performance-enhancing  drugs.
Disseminate educational materials to athletes, coaches, and support staff.
iv. Regulation  and  Enforcement : Formulate  and  implement  anti-doping  rules  and  regulations  in
India. Monitor compliance with anti-doping code of WADA .
7 .6.2  Several  challenges  are  faced  in  the  efforts  to  maintain  a  doping-free  environment  in  sports.
These  challenges  arise  from  various  factors,  including  the  evolving  nature  of  doping  practices.
Many  athletes,  especially  at  the  grassroots  level,  are  unaware  of  banned  substances  and  inadvertent
doping  risks  through  contaminated  supplements.  However,  a  variety  of  measures  are  taken  to  ensure
the  accuracy  and  integrity  of  its  testing  procedures.  These  measures  are  designed  to  adhere  to
international  standards  and  guidelines  set  by  the  World  Anti-Doping  Agency  (WADA) . The  Details
are as under: 
i. Compliance with WADA Code
ii. Doping  Control  Officers  (DCOs)  and  chaperones  are  thoroughly  trained  and  certified  to
handle sample collection, ensuring standardized and professional procedure
iii. A strict chain of custody  is maintained to prevent tampering or mismanagement of samples
iv. Samples  are  collected  under  standardized  conditions,  with  athletes  observed  by  trained
personnel to prevent sample adulteration or substitution.
v. Sample collection kits are tamper-proof and sealed in the athlete's presence.
vi. Athletes  are  educated  about  the  testing  process  to  ensure  they  understand  their  rights,
responsibilities, and the importance of compliance.
7 .6.3 By  implementing  above  measures,  it  is  ensured  that  testing  procedures  are  accurate,  fair,
thereby upholding the integrity of sports and protecting clean athletes. 
7 .6.4 The  Committee  was  informed  that  a llocation  to  the  National  Anti-Doping  Agency  has  been
reduced  from  ₹ 24.30  crores  in  RE  (2025 – 26)  to  ₹ 20.30  crores  in  BE  (2026 – 27),  while  allocation  to
the  National  Dope  Testing  Laboratory  has  declined  from  ₹ 28.55  crores  to  ₹ 23.00  crores  during  the
same period. 
7 .6. 5 NADA  plays  a  crucial  role  in  educating  athletes,  coaches  and  support  staff  about  anti-
doping rules to promote fair play and compliance. The details of such measures are as under:
i. Workshops,  seminars,  and  training  programs  are  conducted  across  various  regions  and
sports  disciplines.  these  sessions  are  tailored  for  athletes,  coaches,  medical  staff,  and  other
support personnel.

84
ii. Campaigns  are  runningto  promote  a  doping-free  culture,  emphasizing  the  importance  of
ethical sportsmanship.
iii. E-learning modules and resources are promoted through the official website of NADA
iv. The  use  of  the  Anti-Doping  Education  and  Learning  (ADEL)  platform  is  encouraged  for
global anti-doping education.
v. Materials  such  as  the  Anti-Doping  Rulebook,  TUE  guidelines,  and  supplement  advisories
are  published  by  NADA.  These  materials  are  often  translated  into  regional  languages  to
ensure broader reach.
vi. The updated WADA Prohibited List is disseminated by NADA annually.
vii. Athletes  are  educated  about  the  risks  of  contaminated  or  unverified  supplements  and
encouraged to consult experts before use.
viii. Athletes  and  medical  staff  are  informed  about  the  process  of  applying  for  TUEs  for
medications containing prohibited substances through workshops.
ix. NADA  partners  with  national  sports  federations,  state  associations,  and  educational
institutions to organize joint awareness initiatives.
x. Social media platforms are leveraged to disseminate information quickly and effectively.
7 .6.6 NADA  and  NDTL  play  a  crucial  role  in  guiding  and  regulating  sportspersons  to  ensure  fair
play,  integrity,  and  adherence  to  anti-doping  rules.  Its  effectiveness  in  guiding  athletes  is  based  on
Education  and  Awareness  Programs,  Anti-Doping  Rules  &  Compliance,  Testing  &  Monitoring,
Support  and  Guidance  for  Athletes,  Handling  Violations  and  Legal  Support.  The  Agency  and  the
Laboratory  continue  to  conduct  testing,  awareness  programmes  and  scientific  analysis  effectively  in
accordance  with  prescribed  standards.  Necessary  steps  are  being  taken  to  address  emerging
challenges  and  to  further  strengthen  the  anti-doping  framework  in  the  country.   The  total  number  of
Anti-Doping Rule Violations (ADRVs) from 2018 through December 31, 2025 are  given below:

7 .6.7 T he  Committee  was  informed  that  the  B E  a llocation  for  NADA  has  been  kept  at  ₹ 20.30
crores  and  NDTL  at  ₹ 23.00  crores  for  the  FY  2026-27  as  per  the  assessed  requirements  and  overall
budgetary  provisions.  It  is  also  informed  that  the  present  allocation  is  considered  adequate  to  meet

85
India ’s  testing,  education  and  compliance  obligations  under  the  World  Anti-Doping  Code.  Further,
additional budgetary requirement, if any, will be considered at the RE stage.
7 .6.8  The  Committee  not es  that  the  National  Anti-Doping  Agency  (NADA)  plays  a  crucial
role  in  maintaining  the  integrity  of  Indian  sports  through  testing,  results  management,
regulatory  enforcement  and  extensive  education  and  awareness  generation  initiatives.  While
the  Agency  has  instituted  robust  mechanisms  in  compliance  with  the  World  Anti-Doping
Code,  including  trained  Doping  Control  Officers,  strict  chain-of-custody  procedures  and
dissemination  of  educational  materials  in  regional  languages,  the  data  on  Anti-Doping  Rule
Violations  (ADRVs)  indicates  fluctuating  trends  and  continuous  violations  in  recent  years.
The  Committee,  therefore,  recommends  that  NADA  should  take  targeted  initiatives , sport-
specific  and  grassroots-level  preventive  education al  programmes,  particularly  in  high-
incidence  disciplines  and  vulnerable  regions,  with  a  focus  on  supplement  awareness,
inadvertent  doping  risks  and  plann ed  engagement  through  federations  and  training
academies. 
7 .6.9 The  Committee  notes  that  the  Budget  Estimates  for  both  NADA  and  the  National  Dope
Testing  Laboratory  (NDTL)  for  FY  2026 – 27  has  reduced  compared  to  the  Revised  Estimates
of  FY  2025 – 26,  even  though  anti-doping  monitoring  responsibilities  and  international
compliance  obligations  continue  to  expand  as  cases  of  Anti-Doping  Rule  violation  cases
continue  to  occur  as  evident  from  Table  no.  36 . Although  the  allocation  is  stated  to  be
adequate  at  the  BE  stage,  continues  testing , technological  upgradation  and  expanded  outreach
require  stable  financial  support . The  Committee  recommends  that  the  Ministry  closely
monitor  fund  utilisation  and  testing  coverage  during  the  financial  year  should  be  monitored
and  ensure  timely  augmentation  of  funds  at  the  Revised  Estimates  stage,  if  warranted,  so  that
financial  constraints  do  not  adversely  impact  testing  frequency  or  international  compliance
commitments.
7 .7  NATIONAL SPORTS UNIVERSITY
7 .7.1 The  Committee  was  briefed  that  the  University,  once  developed,  will  be  the  first  one  of  its
kind  to  promote  sports  education  in  the  areas  of  sports  sciences,  sports  technology,  sports
management,  &  sports  coaching.  It  will  also  function  as  the  national  training  centre  for  selected
sports  disciplines  by  adopting  the  best  international  practices  by  signing  Memoranda  of
Understanding  (MoU ) with  international  universities.  The  Ministry  of  Youth  Affairs  &  Sports  has
already  signed  MoUs  with  the  Universities  of  Canberra  &  Universities  of  Victoria  regar ding
involving  the  University.  The  University  is  ordained  to  impart  undergraduate,  postgraduate,  and
doctoral  programmes  in  Sports  Coaching,  Sports  Sciences,  and  Physical  Education  in  various
disciplines.  Specialised  degree  programmes  in  various  disciplines  of  Sports  Education  and
Coaching  are  also  envisaged  for  the  future.  NSU  shall  focus  on  collaborating  with  leading
researchers  within  and  outside  the  country  to  undertake  pioneering  research  programmes  and
exchange  information  in  various  research  areas,  particularly  in  the  field  of  sports  coaching  and
performance enhancement of athletes.
7 .7.2 The  University  promotes  innovation  in  sports  science  and  technology  through  various
initiatives,  such  as  research  grants  and  collaborations  with  industry  partners.  The  University
encourages  students  and  faculty  to  engage  in  research  projects  that  focus  on  developing  new

86
technologies  and  methodologies  in  sports  science.  Further,  the  faculty  of  University  has  established
collaborations  with  research  institutions  and  the  Ministry  to  advance  its  research  agenda  to  integrate
scientific  knowledge  from  laboratories  into  real  time  sports  settings,  strengthening  NSU ’s  academic
and research profile at National and global levels.
The academic programme offered by National Sports University, Manipur are as follows:

7 .7.3 The  National  Sports  University  is  currently  functioning  from  temporary  campus  at
KhumanLampak  Sports  Complex,  Imphal.  The  University  provides  students  with  a  comprehensive
and  contemporary  learning  atmosphere  by  integrating  advanced  technology  and  inventive  methods.
The University has state-of-the-art facilities for sports training and education, including:
 Modern  sports  infrastructure  and  training  facilities  for  various  sports  disciplines  including
spaces  for  indoor  and  outdoor  sports,  a  well-  equipped  gym,  swimming  pool  and  multi-
purpose hall.
 Well-equipped  laboratories  for  sports  science  research,  including  computer  labs,  Sports
Psychology  labs,  Sports  Nutrition  Research  Lab,  Sports  and  Exercise  Physiology  Lab,  and
Anthropometry Lab.

87
 Library  and  resource  center  with  a  vast  collection  of  books,  journals,  and  online  resources
related  to  Sports  Science  (Sports  Psychology,  Sports  Nutrition,  and  Exercise  Physiology)
and physical education.
 Computer labs facilities for data analytics and video analysis.
7 .7. 4 The  Committee  was  informed  that  to  address  the  infrastructure  requirements  and  potential
issues,  the  University  has  developed  a  comprehensive  strategy  centered  on  professional  Operation
&  Maintenance  (O&M)  and  the  completion  of  state-of-the-art  utility  systems.  Further,  the
University  is  in  regular  coordination  with  the  State  Government  and  respective  stakeholders  for
addressing  various  issues  relating  to  infrastructure.  The  University  has  established  partnerships  with
several  professional  sports  teams  and  academies  to  provide  students  with  hands-on  experience  and
exposure  to  industry ’s  best  practices.  Further,  the  challenges  of  maintaining  and  upgrading  its
facilities,  the  university  has  implemented  a  comprehensive  maintenance  plan,  which  includes
regular inspections, repairs, and upgrades to ensure that the facilities remain modern and functional.
7 .7. 5    The  Committee  notes  that  the  National  Sports  University  (NSU),  envisioned  as  a
premier  institution  for  sports  education,  research  and  training,  has  made  progress  in
launching  specialised  undergraduate,  postgraduate  and  doctoral  programmes  in  Sports
Coaching,  Sports  Sciences,  Sports  Psychology  and  Applied  Sports  Nutrition.  The  University ’s
collaborations  with  reputed  international  universities  and  national  research  institutions,  along
with  its  focus  on  innovation  and  integration  of  laboratory  research  into  real-time  sports
settings,  reflect  its  potential  to  emerge  as  a  centre  of  excellence.  However,  the  Committee
not es  that  the  University  continues  to  function  from  a  temporary  campus  at  Khuman  Lampak
Sports  Complex,  Imphal.  The  Committee,  therefore,  recommends  that  priority  to  the
expeditious  completion  and  operationalisation  of  the  permanent  campus  with  world-class
academic,  research  and  residential  infrastructure  should  be  accorded , so  that  the  University
may  fully  realise  its  mandate  as  a  national  training  centre  and  global  hub  for  sports  education
and research.
7 .7. 6 The  Committee  further  notes  that  while  NSU  has  developed  modern  laboratories,
sports  infrastructure  and  industry  partnerships,  the  intake  capacity  across  programmes
remains  relatively  modest  considering  the  national  demand  for  qualified  sports  scientists,
coaches  and  support  professionals.  In  order  to  strengthen  India ’s  sports  ecosystem  move  in  a
fruitful  manner,  the  Committee  recommends  that  a  phased  expansion  plan  for  increasing
student  intake,  diversifying  specialised  disciplines,  and  enhancing  faculty  strength,  alongside
sustainable  Operation  &  Maintenance  mechanisms  should  be  formulated  for  better
development  of  Sports  ecosystem  in  the  country . Greater  integration  with  National  Centres  of
Excellence,  Khelo  India  Centres  and  sports  federations  may  also  be  institutionalised  to  ensure
that  academic  output  directly  contributes  to  athlete  performance  enhancement  and  capacity
building across the country.
7 .8 PENSION TO MERITORIOUS SPORTS PERSONS
7 .8.1 The  Committee  notes  that  the  Department  has  provisioned  for  post-retirement  support  for
ex-sportspersons  who  have  brought  laurels  to  the  country,  and  that  over  850  sportspersons  have
been  awarded  lifelong  pension  ranging  up  to  Rs.  20,000/-  per  month.  The  Committee  observes  that

88
the  career  of  sports  persons  and  their  pensions  should  be  made  lucrative  so  that  the  required  talent  is
attracted  and  retained  properly.  Accordingly,  the  Committee  recommends  that  the  pension  due  to
meritorious sportspersons be not less than the pension accorded to a retired Class 1 Officer .

Representation/ reservation policy for women, ST/SC and OBC is followed as per Government of 
India's norms. 
8 .6 The  Committee  notes  that  the  overall  representation  of  W omen  and  other  categories  in
the  Main  Secretariat  and  in  key  technical  and  scientific  institutions  such  as  NADA,  NDTL  and
NSU  including  SAI  is  not  encouraging . The  Committee,  therefore,  recommends  that  a
comprehensive  recruitment  policy  may  be  formulated  with  measurable  targets  and  periodic
review  mechanisms  to  enhance  representation  of  women  and  other  categories  in  various  fields
required for development of Sports ecosystem in the country .
8 .7 The  Committee  further  observes  variations  in  representation  of  SC,  ST,  OBC  and
Persons  with  Disabilities  (PWD)  across  organisations , with  comparatively  low  PWD
representation  and  certain  shortfalls  in  reserved  categories  in  specific  bodies.  While
reservation  policies  are  stated  to  be  followed  as  per  Government  norms,  the  Committee
recommends  that  a  detailed  reservation  roster  audit  be  undertaken  across  all  institutions

91
under  the  Department  of  Sports  to  identify  backlog  vacancies,  if  any,  and  to  initiate  special
recruitment  drives  to  ensure  full  compliance  with  statutory  reservation  provisions  and
inclusive workforce participation.
8 .8 The  Committee  notes  the  considerable  reliance  on  contractual  appointments  over  the
past  five  years,  particularly  in  SAI  and  NADA,  where  contractual  engagement  appears
significantly  higher  than  permanent  recruitment.  While  contractual  hiring  may  pr ovide
operational  flexibility,  continuous  dependence  on  such  arrangements  may  affect  institutional
memory,  continuity  of  programmes  and  accountability  structures.  The  Committee
recommends  that  a  manpower  rationalisation  exercise  should  be  undertaken  to  assess  core
versus  project-based  requirements  and  progressively  regularise  essential  positions  through
sanctioned permanent posts, thereby ensuring long-term organisational  stability.
8 .9 The  Committee  also  observes  that  permanent  recruitment  in  several  institutions  such
as  NSU,  NDTL  and  LNIPE  has  been  limited  in  recent  years,  which  may  constrain  academic
expansion,  research  output  and  technical  efficiency.  The  Committee  recommends  that
recruitment  processes  be  streamlined  and  expedited  in  coordination  with  the  competent
authorities so that sanctioned posts  are filled in a time-bound manner .
8 .10 The  Committee  notes  about  the  vacancy  position  at  LNIPE,  NERC,  Guwahati ,
including  the  vacant  Professor  post  and  two  Assistant  Professor  posts . Given  the  critical  role
of  faculty  in  maintaining  academic  excellence,  research  supervision  and  curriculum
development,  the  Committee  recommends  that  immediate  steps  be  taken  to  fill  these  vacancies
and  to  periodically  review  sanctioned  strength  in  light  of  expanding  academic  programmes
and student intake.
8 .11 The  Committee  recommends  that  a  centralised  Human  Resource  Monitoring  and
Planning  Framework  within  the  Department  of  Sports  may  be  established , incorporating
sanctioned  strength,  persons  in  position,  vacancies,  category-wise  representation,  contractual
engagements  and  recruitment  timelines  across  all  subordinate  bodies.  Such  a  structured
monitoring  mechanism  would  enhance  transparency,  facilitate  evidence-based  workforce
planning  and  ensure  optimal  deployment  of  human  resources  in  furtherance  of  the
Department ’s expanding mandate and long-term strategic objectives.

92
OVERALL  ASSESSMENT  OF  THE  DEMANDS  FOR  GRANTS  (2026 -27)  OF  THE
DEPARTMENT OF YOUTH AFFAIRS
1. From  figures  given  at  Table  3,  it  is  evident  that  Rs.  1,273.77  crores  has  been  allocated
under  BE  2025-26  which  has  been  decreased  to  Rs.999.40  crores  at  RE  stage.  The  Committee
observes  that  this  reduction  at  RE  stage  amounts  to  Rs.274.37  crores  which  is  nearly  21.54%
less  in  comparison  to  allocation  made  at  BE  2025-26.  Further,  the  Committee  notes  that  the
Actual  expenditure  of  the  Department  is  only  Rs.  696.68  crores  as  on  31.01.2026  which  is  only
69.71%  of  the  reduced  allocations  of  RE  2025-26.  In  this  regard,  the  Committee  would  like  to
recommend  the  Department  to  utilize  funds  timely,  so  that,  no  rush  for  expenditure
particularly in the closing quarter of financial year.
(Para 2.9)
2. The  Committee  observes  that  there  is  a  variation  between  the  projections  and  allocation
in  BE  2026-27  of  -20.74  per  cent.  In  order  to  ensure  that  the  scheme  which  are  vital  for  the
benefit  of  Youth  does  not  suffer  due  to  shortage  of  funds.  The  Committee  recommends  that
additional  funds  may  be  sought  at  the  time  of  RE  2026-27  to  ensure  proper  implementation  of
schemes.
(Para 2.13)
3. The  Committee  takes  note  of  the  vacancies  in  Secretariat- DoYA  and  observes  that  61
posts  are  vacant  against  the  sanctioned  strength  240.  The  Committee  also  observes  that  4 ,32  and
25  posts  of  Group  A,  Group  B  and  Group  C,  respectively,  are  vacant.  The  Committee,  therefore,
recommends  the  Department  to  complete  the  process  of  filling  up  the  vacancies  in  a  time-bound
manner  and  latest  by  the  end  of  2026,  so  that,  implementation  of  schemes  and  programmes  of
the Department does not suffer and its benefits reach down to the grass root level effectively.
(Para 2.22)
4. The  sharp  82.37  per  cent  increase  in  BE  2026-27  over  RE  2025-26  indicates  a
substantial  contraction  in  spending  during  the  previous  fiscal  year.  The  Committee  is  of  the
view  that  a  Department  level  audit  may  be  conducted  to  identify  the  grey  areas  that  led  to  the
low utilization of RE 2025-26.  
(Para 3.2.3)
5. During  the  course  of  the  meeting,  the  Department  has  informed  that  the  sanctioned
strength  for  MY  Bharat  Youth  Volunteers  under  the  NYC  scheme  is  13,206 . However,  targets
for  the  2026-27  financial  year  indicate  a  plan  to  deploy  exactly  this  sanctioned  amount,
suggesting  that  any  existing  gaps  must  be  closed  immediately  to  ensure  full  operational
capacity . The  Committee  is  of  the  view  that  the  Department  should  ensure  100  per  cent
deployment  against  the  sanctioned  strength  of  13,206  volunteers  and  time-bound  recruitment
and  deployment  calendar  should  also  be  strictly  followed  to  prevent  any  service  gaps  at  the
block and district levels.
(Para 3.2.4)

93
6. The  Committee  was  apprised  that  the  Department  of  Youth  Affairs  is  planning  for  a
significant  expansion  of  the  NYC  volunteer  base,  increasing  placement  from  6,600  blocks  to
7,319  blocks  in  the  country . Additionally,  there  is  a  proposal  to  deploy  one  volunteer  as  a
District  Level  Coordinator  in  each  of  the  786  districts . The  Committee,  therefore,
recommends  the  Department  to  prioritize  the  deployment  of  these  District  Level  Coordinators
to  bridge  the  gap  between  block-level  activities  and  district-level  administration  as  this
structure  will  improve  monitoring  and  reporting  of  grassroot  level  initiatives  through  the  MY
Bharat platform.
(Para 3.2.5)
7. The  Committee  notes  that  under  NYC  volunteers  serve  for  an  initial  period  of  one
year,  extendable  to  a  maximum  of  two  years . As  a  result,  there  is  a  need  to  track  the
professional  and  academic  progress  of  these  volunteers  after  their  term  ends  to  assess  the
long-term  impact  of  the  scheme . The  Committee  recommends  that  in  order  to  leverage  the
existing  MY  Bharat  Portal,  which  already  provides  dedicated  profile  pages  and  digital
credentials  for  youth , the  Department  should  develop  a  alumni  tracking  module  for  NYC
volunteers  that  will  help  inform  future  policy  adjustments  and  create  a  valuable  alumni
network for peer mentoring and professional opportunities .
(Para 3.2.6)
8. The  Committee  notes  that  the  Department  recognizes  of  Youth  Affairs  "Adolescents"
(age  10-19)  as  a  distinct  and  vital  subset  of  the  youth  population.  However,  the  budget  for
NPYAD  is  often  grouped  under  broader  youth  leadership  heads.  The  Committee,  therefore,
recommends  that  a  dedicated  sub-component  or  a  ring-fenced  budget  within  NPYAD
specifically  for  adolescent-focused  interventions  should  be  created  and  must  include  life-skills
education  and  counseling  services  to  address  the  unique  psychological  and  physiological
transitions  of  this  age  group,  ensuring  that  they  are  not  overshadowed  by  the  15-29  age
bracket .
(Para 3.3.2)
9. The  Committee  was  informed  that  a  restructuring  of  NPYAD  is  underway  to
accommodate  the  Viksit  Bharat  Young  Leaders  Dialogue  (VBYLD),  with  the  budget  for
NPYAD  increasing  to  ₹ 58.41  crores  in  BE  2026-27  (up  from  ₹ 40  crores  in  BE  2025-26).  The
Committee  is  of  the  view  that  as  this  dialogue  is  not  limited  to  State  capitals  only,  given  the  46
per  cent  increase  in  budgetary  allocation,  The  program  should  be  decentralized  to  the  district
level  to  identify  and  mentor  "Change  Makers"  from  rural  and  tribal  backgrounds,  providing
them a direct platform to contribute to the vision of a developed India by 2047.
(Para 3.3.3)
10. The  Committee  recommends  the  Department  to  implement  a  mandatory  minimum  33
per  cent  quota  for  female  participation  in  all  NPYAD-funded  adventure  treks  and  leadership
camps.  Further,  specialized  "All-Women"  leadership  workshops  should  be  introduced  to
encourage  participation  from  regions  where  cultural  barriers  might  otherwise  limit  mixed-
gender engagement.

94
(Para 3.3.4)
11. The  Committee  notes  that  since  NPYAD  aims  at  "Personality  Development,"  the
current  activity  list  is  heavily  weighted  toward  events  and  camps.  The  Committee,  therefore
recommends  Department  that  a  portion  of  the  NPYAD  budget  should  be  utilized  to  provide
small-scale  "Youth  Innovation  Grants"  for  community-based  research  projects.  By
partnering  with  RGNIYD,  the  Department  can  encourage  youth  to  conduct  local  surveys  or
develop  social  innovation  prototypes,  thereby  shifting  the  scheme's  focus  from  mere
"participation" to "problem-solving."
(Para 3.3.5)
12. The  Committee  notes  that  NPYAD  involves  providing  grants  to  Voluntary
Organizations  (NGOs)  for  youth  development  activities  but  outstanding  Utilization
Certificates  (UCs)  remain  a  major  concern  for  the  Department  of  Youth  Affairs.  The
Committee  recommends  to  move  toward  a  100  per  cent  digital  monitoring  system  for  NPYAD
grants.  Future  funding  to  NGOs  should  be  strictly  contingent  upon  the  submission  of  geo-
tagged  photos  and  video  evidence  of  the  programs  conducted,  alongside  the  timely  filing  of
UCs  and  will  help  ensure  that  the  increased  outlay  of  ₹ 58.41  crores  in  BE  2026-27  translates
into verifiable grassroots impact.
(Para 3.3.6)
13. The  Committee  notes  that  the  Department  has  been  increasingly  involved  in
multilateral  forums  such  as  the  G20  (Y20),  BRICS,  and  SCO.  While  these  platforms  offer
immense  visibility,  the  current  budgetary  allocation  must  reflect  India's  aspiration  to  lead  the
global  youth  agenda.  The  Committee,  therefore,  recommends  to  seek  a  sustained  increase  in
the  "International  Cooperation"  head  at  the  Revised  Estimates  (RE)  stage  for  the  Financial
Year  2026-27,  which  will  ensure  that  India  can  host  high-quality  bilateral  and  multilateral
exchanges without compromising on the frequency of delegations.
(Para 3.4.2)
14. The  Committee  further  notes  that  the  selection  process  involves  various  tiers  of
screening  for  representation  in  international  youth  delegations.  The  Committee,  therefore,
recommends  that  the  entire  application  and  selection  process  for  International  Youth
Exchange  Programmes  (IYEP)  should  be  fully  integrated  into  the  MY  Bharat  Portal  which
would  provide  a  transparent,  merit-based  automated  shortlisting  system  based  on  the  "digital
credentials"  and  "verified  volunteering  hours"  of  the  youth  and  ensure  that  opportunities
reach deserving candidates from remote districts.
(Para 3.4.3)
15. The  Committee  has  earlier  emphasized  that  international  visits  should  not  be  "one-
off"  events  but  should  contribute  to  the  national  knowledge  pool.  The  Committee
recommends  that  a  "Post-Visit  Action  Report"  system  is  institutionalised  and  every  returning
delegate  should  be  mandated  to  conduct  at  least  three  "Knowledge  Sharing  Workshops"  in
their  local  NSS  units  or  MY  Bharat  hubs.  Furthermore,  an  annual  impact  assessment  study
should  be  commissioned  through  RGNIYD  to  evaluate  how  these  international  exposures
have influenced the career trajectories and leadership roles of the participants.

95
(Para 3.4.4)
16. It  is  currently  believed  that  the  International  Cooperation  is  focused  heavily  on
cultural  exchange  and  government-to-government  (G2G)  visits.  The  Committee  recommends
that  the  Department  should  broaden  the  scope  of  International  Cooperation  by  facilitating
Memorandum  of  Understandings  between  RGNIYD  and  leading  international  youth  research
institutes  and  should  focus  on  academic  credits  for  youth  work,  joint  research  on  adolescent
psychology,  and  faculty  exchange,  thereby  upgrading  the  domestic  academic  framework  for
youth development to global standards.
(Para 3.4.5)
17. The  Committee  observes  that  out  of  the  85  Youth  Hostels  constructed  across  the
country,  several  are  in  a  state  of  disrepair  or  are  underutilized.  While  BE  2026-27  has
allocated  ₹ 19.20  crores  for  Youth  Hostels  (a  significant  increase  from  the  ₹ 1.1  crores  in  RE
2025-26),  the  focus  should  shift  toward  comprehensive  modernization  of  Youth  Hostels.  The
Committee  recommends  that  the  BE  of  ₹ 19.20  crores  outlay  should  be  utilized  for  the  major
repair,  renovation,  and  "facelift"  of  identified  hostels,  particularly  those  in  heritage  or  high-
tourist  traffic  areas.  Modernization  should  include  digital  connectivity  (Wi-Fi),  refurbished
dormitories,  and  energy-efficient  infrastructure  to  make  them  appealing  to  contemporary
youth travelers.
(Para 3.5.2)
18. The  Committee  observes  that  most  of  the  Youth  Hostels  function  merely  as  low-cost
lodging  facilities  and  feels  that  these  spaces  should  serve  a  larger  purpose  in  youth
development  of  the  country.  The  Committee,  therefore,  recommends  that  functional  mandate
of  Youth  Hostels  should  be  redefined  by  transforming  them  into  "Youth  Resource  Hubs."
These  hubs  should  host  career  counseling  centers,  digital  skill-training  workshops,  and
localized  MY  Bharat  registration  desks,  ensuring  the  infrastructure  is  utilized  throughout  the
day rather than just for overnight stays.
(Para 3.5.3)
19. The  Committee  observes  that  a  few  State  Governments  have  diverted  Youth  Hostel
premises  for  administrative  offices  or  other  non-youth-related  purposes.  The  Committee
recommends  that  a  physical  audit  of  all  85  hostels  should  be  conducted  to  find  out  the  current
usage.  A  strict  "Usage  Protocol"  should  be  enforced,  and  any  State  Government  found
utilizing  these  premises  for  non-scheme  purposes  should  be  issued  a  formal  notice  to  restore
the  facility  to  its  original  mandate  and  subsequent  developmental  grants  for  that  state  under
the RYSK umbrella may be suspended.
(Para 3.5.4)
20. The  Committee  also  notes  that  one  of  the  bottlenecks  in  the  popularity  of  Youth
Hostels  is  the  lack  of  a  seamless,  transparent,  and  modern  booking  mechanism.  The
Committee  recommends  the  Department  to  integrate  a  real-time,  centralized  booking  module
within  the  MY  Bharat  Portal  that  would  allow  youth  to  check  availability  and  book  rooms
across  the  country  using  their  digital  credentials.  Such  a  system  would  also  provide  the

96
Department  with  accurate,  real-time  data  on  occupancy  rates  and  revenue  generation,
facilitating better management.
(Para 3.5.5)
21. The  Committee  notes  that  budgetary  provision  for  Scouting  and  Guiding  has  increased
from  ₹ 0.75  crores  in  BE  2025-26  to  ₹ 3.00  crores  in  BE  2026-27.  However,  given  the  vast
organizational  network  and  the  potential  to  reach  millions  of  young  students  across  rural  and
urban  India,  the  current  outlay  remains  relatively  modest  compared  to  other  youth  programs.
The  Committee  recommends  the  Department  to  evaluate  the  possibility  of  a  further  phased
increase  in  the  allocation  at  the  Revised  Estimates  (RE)  stage  which  would  help  enable  the
organizations  to  scale  up  their  training  programs  and  national-level  jamborees,  ensuring  the
"Scouting movement" reaches every district in the country.
(Para 3.6.2)
22. The  Committee  notes  that  the  Department  of  Youth  Affairs  is  currently  emphasizing
the  MY  Bharat  platform  as  a  single-window  " phygital "  interface  for  all  youth-related
activities.  The  Committee,  therefore,  recommends  to  integrate  all  major  activities,  workshops,
and  certifications  provided  by  Scouting  and  Guiding  organizations  be  registered  and  tracked
through  the  MY  Bharat  Portal.  Integration  would  provide  these  organizations  with  access  to  a
larger  pool  of  volunteers  and  allow  scouts/guides  to  earn  digital  credentials  that  are  part  of  a
unified "Smart CV."
(Para 3.6.3)
23. The  Committee  is  of  the  view  that  a  periodic  third-party  impact  assessment  of  the
scouting  and  guiding  programs  funded  by  Government  should  be  institutionalised  and  future
grants  are  made  performance-linked,  based  on  verifiable  data  such  as  the  number  of
" Rashtrapati  Awardees"  produced,  the  success  of  community  service  projects,  and  the  timely
submission of Utilization Certificates (UCs) etc .
(Para 3.6.4)
24. The  Committee  notes  that  the  Neighborhood  Youth  Parliament  (NYP)  is  a  core
component  of  NYLP,  aimed  at  developing  leadership  and  debating  skills  at  the  grassroots
level.  For  BE  2026-27,  the  allocation  for  NYLP  stands  at  ₹ 10.00  crores,  which  is  significantly
lower  than  the  Department  of  Youth  Affairs  internal  projection  of  approximately  ₹ 17.30
crores  required  for  full-scale  implementation.  The  Committee  recommends  the  Department  to
move  for  an  upward  revision  of  the  NYLP  budget  at  the  Revised  Estimates  (RE)  stage.  A
dedicated  fund  of  ₹ 10  crores  is  insufficient  to  cover  786  districts  effectively  if  the  goal  is  to
make youth parliaments a monthly or quarterly feature rather than a one-time annual event.
(Para 3.7.2)
25. The  Committee  notes  that  MY  Bharat  platform  is  designed  to  provide  "digital
credentials"  to  youth  for  their  social  contributions.  The  Committee,  therefore,  recommends
the  Department  to  automatically  reflect  activities/achievements  of  volunteer  in  the  volunteer ’s
Smart  CV  on  the  MY  Bharat  portal  such  as  participation  in  NYLP  activities,  winning  a
district  or  state-level  Youth  Parliament  etc.  This  would  allow  young  leaders  to  showcase  their

97
leadership  and  public  speaking  credentials  to  potential  employers  or  academic  institutions,
thereby increasing the aspirational  value of the program.
(Para 3.7.3)
26. The  Committee  feels  that  Neighborhood  Youth  Parliaments  serve  as  platforms  for
debate  and  discussion  on  socio-economic  issues.  However,  the  feedback  loop  from  these
discussions  to  the  actual  policy-making  process  remains  informal.  The  Committee,  therefore,
further  recommends  the  Department  to  establish  a  formal  mechanism  where  the  resolutions
or  "Youth  Declarations"  passed  at  State  and  National  Youth  Parliaments  are  compiled  and
shared  with  relevant  Ministries  (such  as  Education,  Health,  or  Rural  Development)  which
would  transform  NYLP  from  a  purely  "personality  development"  exercise  into  a  meaningful
tool for youth-led governance .
(Para 3.7.4)
27. The  committee  was  informed  that  MY  Bharat  (erstwhile  NYKS)  was  allotted  Rs.
423.00  crores  at  RE  2025-26  and  Actual  expenditure  was  Rs.  318.50  crores  as  on  19.01.2026.
Further,  it  was  also  informed  that  shortage  in  manpower  resulted  in  this  underlisation  of
funds.  While  the  BE  for  2026-27  has  been  significantly  increased  to  ₹ 655.22  crores  to  support
the  full  absorption  of  NYKS  staff  and  assets,  persistent  low  utilization  weakens  the  case  for
further  funding . The  Committee,  therefore,  recommends  to  implement  a  robust  institutional
monitoring  framework  to  ensure  optimal  and  timely  utilization  of  the  increased  ₹ 655.22
crores  allocation . Besides,  the  Department  should  make  sincere  e fforts  to  streamline
administrative  processes  for  asset  transfer  from  NYKS  to  MY  Bharat  and  ensuring  that  quarterly
expenditure plans are strictly followed to avoid end-of-year "parking" of funds .
(Para 3.9.9)
28. The  Committee  notes  that  there  is  a  massive  shortage  of  manpower  in  MY  Bharat,
autonomous  body.  As  of  December  31,  2025,  MY  Bharat  has  1,413  vacancies  against  a  sanctioned
strength  of  2,000  posts . Specifically,  key  leadership  and  field  positions  remain  unoccupied,
including  4  Director  posts , 22  Joint/State  Director  posts,  and  44  Deputy  Director  posts . The
Committee  recommends  the  Department  to  launch  a  time-bound  special  recruitment  drive  to
fill  these  1,413  vacancies  and  ensure  that  the  nationwide  expansion  and  operational
effectiveness  of  the  MY  Bharat  platform  are  not  compromised  due  to  a  lack  of  human
resources .
(Para 3.9.12)
29. The  Committee  notes  that  MY  Bharat  has  successfully  crossed  2.07  crores  youth
registrations,  its  primary  mandate  is  to  act  as  a  " phygital "  platform  that  bridges  the  gap
between  digital  opportunities  and  grassroots  engagement.  It  was  also  informed  that  MY
Bharat  (subsuming  NYKS)  is  the  principal  vehicle  for  mobilizing  rural  youth  through  2.58
lakh  village-based  Youth  Clubs.  The  Committee  recommends  to  ensure  that  youth  in  remote
and  marginalized  areas  are  not  excluded  from  the  digital  ecosystem  and  to  prevent  a  digital
divide,  "offline"  outreach  mechanisms  should  be  strengthen  and  should  include  increasing  the
number  of  Future  Youth  Leaders  Bootcamps  and  workshops  on  flagship  schemes  specifically
in under-represented districts.

98
(Para 3.9.15)
30. The  Committee  is  of  the  view  that  MY  Bharat  connects  youth  clubs  throughout  the
country.  These  clubs,  therefore,  should  be  empowered  with  "Campaign  kits"  (digital  and
physical)  to  host  street  plays,  Marathons,  or  Nukad  Nataks  specifically  during  local  festivals
when  substance  use  may  hike.  Use  of  geo-location  data  to  identify  directly  with  high  youths
registration  but  low  "drug  free"  activity  engagement  would  also  help  identify  high  rist  aones
and better use of resources.
(Para 3.9.16)
31. The  Committee  notes  that  MY  Bharat  serves  as  the  executing  agency  for  the  National
Youth  Corps  (NYC)  scheme,  where  volunteers  receive  an  honorarium  of  ₹ 5,000  per  month .
The  Committee  notes  that  the  volunteer  base  is  under  expansion  and  a  proposal  for  enhanced
honorarium  is  currently  under  consideration  by  the  Department . The  Committee,  therefore,
recommends  that  considering  current  economic  standards  and  the  critical  role  these
volunteers  play  in  grassroots  nation-building  and  disaster  response,  the  proposal  to  increase
the  monthly  honorarium  beyond  the  existing  ₹ 5,000  to  attract  higher-quality  talent  should  be
extended as well as to ensure the long-term sustainability of the volunteer network .
(Para 3.9.17)
32. The  Committee  notes  the  vacancy  positions  within  the  administrative  and  operational
framework  of  the  National  Service  Scheme  (NSS).  As  of  31st  December  2025,  the  Department
reported  that  out  of  a  total  sanctioned  strength  of  199  posts  across  various
categories — including  Group  ‘A ’ (Gazetted ),  Group  ‘B ’ (Gazetted  and  Non- Gazetted ),  and
Group  ‘C ’— a  staggering  120  posts  remain  vacant  constitute  an  overall  vacancy  rate  of
approximately  60.30  per  cent,  a  situation  where  it  is  difficult  to  manage  for  a  premier  national
youth  organization.  Further,  to  support  the  proposed  addition  of  25,000  new  NSS  units  in  the
2026-27  cycle , the  Committee  recommends  to  ensure  that  Regional  Directorates  are  staffed  at
100  per  cent  capacity.  Since,  administrative  staff  at  the  regional  level  are  the  primary  link
between  the  Ministry  and  educational  institutions,  the  Committee  recommends  to  initiate  an
emergency,  time-bound  recruitment  process  to  fill  all  120  vacant  positions  latest  by  31 st
December,  2026  as  without  this  the  objective  of  education  through  service  cannot  be
monitored  effectively.  The  current  reliance  on  a  mere  39.70  per  cent  of  the  sanctioned
workforce  is  unsustainable  and  severely  compromises  the  Department's  ability  to  provide
effective oversight and guidance to its millions of volunteers.
(Para 3.10.7)
33. The  Committee  notes  that  various  states  have  taken  initiative  to  reward  and  recognize
the  contributions  of  NSS  volunteers,  but  that  there  is  a  wide  variation  in  the  policy  adopted  by
different  states.  The  Committee  recommends  to  bridge  this  gap  by  developing  and  radiating  a
common  framework  for  the  same.  In  this  regard,  the  Committee  recommends  that  the
Department  of  Youth  Affairs  should  work  with  the  Department  of  Higher  Education  to
ensure  that  service  hours  undertaken  by  NSS  volunteers  are  creditized  in  the  Academic  Bank
of Credits accepted by HEIs. 
(Para 3.10.8)

99
34. The  Committee  recognises  the  pivotal  role  of  the  Rajiv  Gandhi  National  Institute  of
Youth  Development  (RGNIYD)  as  an  Institute  of  National  Importance  and  the  premier
"think-tank"  for  youth-related  activities  in  the  country.  While  the  Budget  Estimate  (BE)  for
2026-27  is  set  at  ₹ 26.50  crores — a  marginal  increase  from  the  previous  year — the  Committee
believes  this  allocation  may  be  insufficient  to  meet  the  Institute ’s  expanding  national  mandate.
The  Committee  is  of  the  view  that  as  an  Institute  of  National  Importance,  RGNIYD  should
not  be  constrained  by  a  "marginal  increase"  in  funding  under  BE  for  FY  2026-27.  The
Department  should  present  a  detailed  requirement  for  additional  grants  at  the  RE  stage  to
ensure  RGNIYD  is  financially  sound  to  fully  realize  its  potential  as  a  global  leader  in  youth
development
(Para 3.11.8)
35. The  Committee  notes  that  there  is  a  "shortage  of  manpower"  as  a  primary  challenge
in  implementing  youth-related  programs . As  of  31 st  December,  2025,  the  sanctioned  strength
for  the  RGNIYD  is  65 , h owever  the  actual  number  of  employees  in  position  remains
significantly  lower  at  43  which  indicates  a  persistent  vacancy  rate  of  approximately  33.84  per
cent.  Further,  there  is  100  per  cent  vacancy  for  the  post  of  Director,  Registrar,  Professors,
Controller  of  Examination  and  Finance  Officer  even  after  the  Committee  highlighted  the
same  in  its  Report  No  366.  RGNIYD  is  notified  as  an  'Institute  of  National  Importance,'  the
lack  of  filled  academic  and  administrative  positions  hinders  its  role  as  a  "think-tank"  and  a
mentor  for  the  nationwide  networks  of  MY  Bharat  and  NSS . The  Committee,  therefore,
recommends  to  fill-up  all  the  existing  vacancies  in  a  time-bound  manner  and  not  later  that
31 st  December, 2026 and apprise  the Committee of the same.
(Para 3.11.10)
OVERALL  ASSESSMENT  OF  THE  DEMANDS  FOR  GRANTS  (2026-27)  OF  THE
DEPARTMENT OF SPORTS
36. The  Committee  notes  the  sharp  upward  revision  in  Revenue  expenditure  from
₹ 2,347.14  crore  at  the  Budget  Estimates  stage  to  ₹ 3,755.26  crore  at  the
Revised  Estimates  stage  during  2025 – 26.  While  such  enhancement  may  reflect
accelerated  implementation  of  schemes  and  additional  commitments,  the
Committee  is  of  the  view  that  the  Department  should  strengthen  its  budget
expenditure  planning  mechanisms  to  minimize  substantial  mid-year  revisions
and proper utilization of the Budgetary allocation for the FY 2026-27.
(Para 5.7)
37. The  Committee  observes  that  the  absence  of  fresh  Capital  allocation  in  FY
2025 – 26,  indicating  a  possible  shift  toward  consolidation  of  existing
infrastructure  and  emphasis  on  revenue-driven  execution.  While  prioritizing
program  delivery  is  commendable,  the  Committee  recommends  that  the
Department  ensure  that  critical  capital  investments  are  not  deferred  in  a
manner  that  may  adversely  affect  long-term  capacity  building  and  asset
creation.  A  balanced  approach  between  Revenue  and  Capital  expenditure

100
should  be  maintained  to  ensure  sustainable  growth  and  institutional
strengthening.
(Para 5.8)
38. With  regard  to  FY  2026 – 27,  the  Committee  appreciates  the  near-alignment
between  the  Department ’ s  projected  Revenue  requirement  of  ₹ 3,111.91  crore
and  the  approved  Budget  Estimate  of  ₹ 3,113.36  crore,  which  reflects
improved  coordination  with  the  Ministry  of  Finance.  The  Committee
recommends  that  this  realistic  and  evidence-based  budgeting  practice  be
institutionalized  through  strengthened  outcome  monitoring,  periodic
expenditure  reviews,  and  better  inter-divisional  coordination,  which  would
ensure optimal utilization of allocated funds.
(Para 5.9)
39. A  comparative  analysis  of  Budget  Estimates  shows  that  BE  2026 – 27  reflects  a
23.52  per  cent  increase  over  BE  2025 – 26,  underscoring  a  continued  upward
trajectory  in  planned  expenditure  and  reaffirming  the  commitment  of  the
Government  to  strengthening  the  sports  ecosystem.  However,  when  compared
with  the  projections  submitted  for  2026 – 27,  the  approved  BE  represents  a
17.09  per  cent  moderation,  indicating  prudent  fiscal  rationalization  at  the
stage  of  budget  finalization.  Overall,  the  financial  pattern  demonstrates  a
sustained  expansion  in  allocations  for  the  sports  sector,  coupled  with  careful
fiscal  calibration.  The  Committee  appreciates  the  trend  as  a  balanced
approach — ensuring enhanced funding support for development.
(Para 5.10)
40. The  Committee  notes  significant  variations  between  Budget  Estimates  (BE)  and  Revised
Estimates  (RE)  in  several  schemes  such  as  National  Centre  for  Sports  Science  and
Research , where  the  RE  2025 – 26  ( ₹ 78.64  crore)  far  exceeds  BE  2025 – 26  ( ₹ 10.00  crore),
and  Khelo  India , where  RE  2025 – 26  ( ₹ 700  crore)  is  substantially  lower  than  BE  2025 – 26
( ₹ 1000  crore).  Such  deviations  indicate  weaknesses  in  expenditure  planning  and
implementation  capacity  has  not  been  either  planned  or  monitored  properly.  The
Committee  is  of  the  view  that  the  Department  should  institute  a  robust  quarterly
expenditure  monitoring  mechanism  and  strengthen  outcome-based  budgeting  to  minimize
sharp fluctuations between BE and RE. 
(Para 5.11)
41. The  allocation  under  Special  Cash  Awards  has  increased  moderately  i.e.  ₹ 36  crore  in  BE
2026 – 27,  while  Pension  to  Meritorious  Sportspersons  shows  a  reduction  from  ₹ 10  crore  at
RE  2025 – 26  to  ₹ 4  crore  at  BE  2026 – 27.  Given  India ’s  expanding  international  sporting
footprint,  sustained  financial  security  for  athletes  is  essential.The  Committee  recommends
creating  a  consolidated  Athlete  Welfare  Corpus  integrating  pension,  medical  assistance,

101
and  emergency  support  under  the  Pandit  Deendayal  Upadhyay  National  Welfare  for
Sportspersons  scheme  to  ensure  long-term  financial  stability  for  retired  and  indigent
sportspersons.  Pension  allocations  should  reflect  realistic  beneficiary  assessments  rather
than reverting to lower BE levels.
(Para 5.12)
