42. The  flagship  Khelo  India  programme  shows  a  decline  from  BE  2025 – 26  ( ₹ 1000  crore)  to
BE  2026 – 27  ( ₹ 924.35  crore),  along  with  underutilisation  in  RE  2025 – 26  ( ₹ 700  crore).  This
may  affect  grassroots  sports  development  and  talent  identification.  The  Department
should  conduct  a  third-party  impact  assessment  of  Khelo  India  verticals,  particularly
infrastructure  creation  and  athlete  scholarships.  Funds  saved  due  to  implementation
bottlenecks  should  be  reallocated  within  the  scheme  to  athlete  development  and  coaching
support to maintain continuity in talent pipelines.
(Para 5.13)
43. The  Committee  observes  sharp  fluctuations  in  allocation  for  National  Centre  for  Sports
Science  and  Research , while  allocations  for  National  Anti-Doping  Agency  ( ₹ 20.30  crore  in
BE  2026 – 27)  have  been  reduced  despite  rising  global  anti-doping  compliance
requirements.  Simultaneously,  contribution  to  World  Anti-Doping  Agency  has  increased.
India ’s  aspiration  to  become  a  global  sporting  power  requires  sustained  investment  in
sports  science  and  anti-doping  infrastructure.  The  Committee  recommends  a  multi-year
funding  roadmap  for  sports  science  hubs  under  a  hub-and-spoke  model  and  restoration
of  adequate  funding  to  NADA  to  ensure  compliance  with  the  World  Anti-Doping  Code
and protect athlete credibility.
(Para 5.14)
44. A  substantial  allocation  of  ₹ 500  crore  has  been  introduced  under  “ Promotion  of  Sports
Goods  Manufacturing .”  While  this  initiative  has  the  potential  to  boost  domestic
manufacturing  and  exports,  the  absence  of  prior  allocation  suggests  the  need  for  clear
operational  guidelines.  The  Department  should  prepare  a  detailed  scheme  framework
outlining  eligibility  criteria,  technology  upgradation  components,  MSME  integration,  and
export  promotion  strategy.  Convergence  with  the  Ministry  of  MSME  and  DPIIT  should
be  ensured  to  avoid  duplication  and  to  maximize  Make-in-India  benefits  in  sports
equipment manufacturing.
(Para 5.15)
45. The  allocation  for  Sports  Authority  of  India  has  increased  to  ₹ 917.38  crore,  while  the
National  Sports  University  shows  a  significant  reduction  from  RE  2025 – 26  ( ₹ 78.64  crore)
to  BE  2026 – 27  ( ₹ 46.98  crore).  Given  the  importance  of  institutional  capacity  in  producing

102
world-class  athletes  and  sports  scientists,  inconsistent  funding  may  hinder  long-term
development.  The  Committee  recommends  that  institutional  grants  be  linked  to  clearly
defined  performance  indicators,  including  medal  outcomes,  athlete  retention,  academic
outputs,  and  infrastructure  utilization.  A  medium-term  expenditure  framework  should  be
adopted  to  ensure  predictable  funding  for  key  institutions  like  SAI,  LNIPE,  and  National
Sports University to support sustained excellence.
(Para 5.16)
46. The  Committee  note  that  the  total  allocation  under  the  Demand  for  Grants  for  the
Department  of  Sports  has  been  placed  at  ₹ 3,113.36  crore  in  BE  2026 – 27  as  against
₹ 2,520.53  crore  in  BE  2025 – 26  and  the  Revised  Estimate  of  ₹ 2,347.14  crore  for  2025 – 26 .
This  reflects  an  overall  increase  of  23.52  per  cent  over  BE  2025 – 26  and  32.65  per  cent
over  RE  2025 – 26 . The  Committee  observe  that  the  substantial  increase  in  allocation
indicates  a  renewed  thrust  on  sports  development,  particularly  in  relation  to
infrastructure  expansion,  preparation  for  major  international  sporting  events  such  as  the
Asian  Games  2026  and  Commonwealth  Games  2026 , strengthening  of  the  anti-doping
framework,  and  expansion  of  flagship  programmes.  The  Committee  view  the  enhanced
outlay  as  a  positive  step  towards  improving  India ’s  competitiveness  in  international
sports.
(Para 5.17)
47. The  Committee  further  note  that  an  allocation  of  ₹ 31.85  crore  has  been  made  under
Secretariat  –  Department  of  Sports  in  BE  2026 – 27 , whereas  no  provision  existed  in  BE
2025 – 26  owing  to  the  earlier  booking  of  establishment  expenditure  under  the  Department
of  Youth  Affairs.  The  present  allocation  reflects  the  splitting  of  establishment  expenditure
between  the  two  Departments . While  the  Committee  acknowledge  that  this  adjustment
improves  administrative  clarity,  they  observe  that  such  restructuring  affects  year-to-year
comparability  of  expenditure.  The  Committee,  therefore,  recommend  that  the  Ministry
provide  detailed  explanatory  notes  in  future  Demands  clearly  outlining  the  implications  of
such changes so as to facilitate accurate analysis of budgetary trends.
(Para 5.18)
48. With  regard  to  institutional  development,  the  Committee  observe  that  the  allocation  to
the  Sports  Authority  of  India  has  increased  moderately  to  ₹ 917.38  crore  in  BE  2026 – 27
from  ₹ 830.00  crore  in  BE  2025 – 26 . The  enhancement  is  stated  to  be  on  account  of  scheme
expansion,  infrastructure  commitments,  equipment  procurement,  athlete  preparation  and
settlement  of  certain  legacy  liabilities.  The  Committee  further  note  that  provisions
relating  to  anti-doping  mechanisms,  including  support  to  the  National  Dope  Testing
Laboratory  and  the  National  Anti-Doping  Agency , have  been  rationalized  in  line  with
operational  requirements  and  the  provisions  of  the  National  Anti-Doping  Act.  While
recognizing  the  importance  of  regulatory  strengthening  and  scientific  testing,  the
Committee  recommend  that  the  Ministry  establish  measurable  outcome  indicators  to
ensure  that  increased  institutional  funding  leads  to  improved  athlete  performance,
enhanced training facilities and strengthened research capabilities.
(Para 5.19)

103
49. The  Committee  also  note  that  the  allocation  to  the  National  Sports  University  has
declined  in  BE  2026 – 27  as  compared  to  both  BE  and  RE  of  the  previous  year,  which  has
been  attributed  to  the  nearing  completion  of  major  capital  works  relating  to  the
permanent  campus  at  Imphal.  While  the  Committee  acknowledge  that  the  reduction  is  in
line  with  the  project  cycle,  they  stress  that  adequate  resources  must  be  ensured  for  the
transition  from  infrastructure  creation  to  full  academic,  research  and  training  operations.
The  Committee  recommend  that  the  Ministry  present  a  comprehensive  operational
roadmap  for  the  University,  including  faculty  recruitment,  development  of  sports  science
programmes and collaboration with national training centres .
(Para 5.20)
50. The  Committee  further  observe  that  allocations  under  schemes  relating  to  encouragement
and  welfare  of  sportspersons  have  witnessed  mixed  trends.  While  provisions  for  Special
Cash  Awards , Human  Resource  Development  in  Sports , and  assistance  to  National  Sports
Federations  have  been  enhanced,  allocations  under  certain  welfare  schemes  remain
modest.  The  Committee  are  of  the  view  that  athlete-centric  support  measures  are  critical
for  sustaining  motivation  and  ensuring  financial  security  of  sportspersons.  The
Committee  therefore  recommend  that  the  Ministry  periodically  review  the  adequacy  of
incentive  schemes  and  strengthen  direct  support  mechanisms  to  ensure  that  outstanding
sportspersons receive timely recognition and financial assistance.
(Para 5.21)
51. The  Committee  note  with  particular  interest  the  introduction  of  a  new  scheme  for
Promotion  of  Sports  Goods  Manufacturing  with  a  substantial  allocation  of  ₹ 500  crore  in
BE  2026 – 27 . The  Committee  view  this  initiative  as  an  important  step  towards
strengthening  the  domestic  sports  ecosystem  and  enhancing  India ’s  capacity  in  sports
equipment  production.  The  Committee  recommend  that  the  scheme  be  implemented  in
close  coordination  with  industry  stakeholders  so  as  to  promote  innovation,  quality
standards and export potential in the sports goods sector.
(Para 5.22)
52. The  Committee  further  observe  that  the  allocation  under  the  Khelo  India  Scheme
remains  substantial  at  ₹ 924.35  crore  in  BE  2026 – 27 , reflecting  the  continued  emphasis  on
grassroots  sports  development,  talent  identification  and  strengthening  of  sports
infrastructure  across  the  country.  The  Committee  also  note  increased  provisions  relating
to  preparations  for  international  competitions  and  associated  infrastructure
commitments.  The  Committee  emphasize  that  effective  monitoring,  timely  completion  of
projects  and  rigorous  evaluation  of  outcomes  are  essential  to  ensure  optimal  utilization  of
public  funds.  The  Committee,  therefore,  recommend  that  the  Ministry  submit  periodic
progress  reports  to  Parliament  detailing  both  physical  and  financial  achievements  under
major schemes and programmes.
(Para 5.23)
53. The  Committee  take  note  of  the  reduction  of  allocation  from  ₹ 37.00  crore  at  BE  2025 – 26
to  ₹ 28.00  crore  at  RE  stage,  primarily  due  to  a  lower  number  of  eligible  applications,  and

104
the  subsequent  increase  to  ₹ 40.00  crore  at  BE  2026 – 27  in  anticipation  of  mega  sporting
events  such  as  the  Asian  Games  and  Commonwealth  Games  in  2026.  While  appreciating
the  performance-linked  and  flexible  character  of  the  scheme,  the  Committee  recommend
that  the  Ministry  may  maintain  a  contingency  buffer  within  the  overall  allocation  to
ensure  that  genuine  claims  are  not  constrained  by  budgetary  ceilings  in  years  of
exceptional  sporting  success.  The  Ministry  may  institute  a  robust  forecasting  mechanism
based  on  upcoming  international  sporting  calendars,  historical  performance  trends,  and
athlete  participation  data  so  as  to  project  fund  requirements  more  realistically  and
minimize sharp variations at the Revised Estimate stage.
(Para 6.1.7)
54. The  Committee  also  notes  that  although  ₹ 46.00  crore  was  projected  for  2026 – 27,  only
₹ 40.00  crore  has  been  approved  at  the  BE  stage,  reflecting  partial  rationalization.  Given
the  importance  of  timely  and  credible  disbursement  of  incentives  to  maintain  athlete
morale,  the  Committee  recommends  that  the  Ministry  may  closely  monitor  the  pace  of
applications  and,  if  required,  seek  supplementary  grants  in  a  timely  manner  so  that  no
eligible sportsperson or coach faces delay in receipt of due incentives.
(Para 6.1.8)
55. The  Committee  also  note  the  substantial  increase  in  allocation  for  Pension  to  Meritorious
Sportspersons  from  ₹ 4.00  crore  to  ₹ 10.00  crore  in  2025 – 26  owing  to  an  increase  in  eligible
beneficiaries.  Considering  the  long-term  welfare  dimension  of  scheme,  the  Committee
recommends  that  the  Ministry  should  periodically  review  pension  rates  and  eligibility
criteria  to  ensure  adequacy  and  sustainability  along  with  strengthening  digital  processing
and  verification  systems  to  have  a  transparent  and  expeditious  disbursement  mechanism
under all sub-components of the Incentives to Sports Persons Scheme.
(Para 6.1.9)
56. The  Committee  notes  that  the  Pandit  Deendayal  Upadhyay  National  Welfare  Fund  for
Sportspersons  is  a  vital  welfare-oriented  intervention  aimed  at  providing  financial
assistance  to  former  and  active  sportspersons  facing  indigent  circumstances,  medical
emergencies,  disability,  or  distress  conditions.  While  observing  that  the  scheme  is
demand-driven  and  that  the  allocation  of  ₹ 2.00  crore  at  BE  2025 – 26  and  BE  2026 – 27  is
considered  adequate  by  the  Ministry,  the  Committee  further  notes  that  the  AE  in  2024 – 25
stood  at  ₹ 0.95  crore  and  the  RE  2025 – 26  was  reduced  to  ₹ 1.00  crore.  The  Committee
recommends  that  the  Ministry  should  undertake  wider  outreach  and  awareness  initiatives
in  coordination  with  National  Sports  Federations  and  State  Governments  to  ensure  that
eligible  beneficiaries  are  adequately  informed  about  the  scheme  and  are  able  to  access
timely assistance.
(Para 6.2.5)

105
57. The  Committee  observes  that  the  scheme  addresses  critical  aspects  of  post-retirement
security,  medical  support,  and  social  dignity  of  sportspersons  who  have  brought  laurels  to
the  nation.  Considering  the  welfare  and  humanitarian  character  of  the  Fund,  the
Committee  recommends  that  the  Ministry  should  periodically  review  the  quantum  of
assistance,  eligibility  norms,  and  processing  timelines  to  enhance  accountability,  and  also
develop  a  technology-enabled  monitoring  system  to  ensure  transparency,  expeditious
disposal of cases, and optimal utilization of allocated funds.
(Para 6.2.6)
58. The  Committee  notes  that  the  National  Sports  Development  Fund  (NSDF)  plays  an
important  role  in  mobilizing  extra-budgetary  resources  for  sports  promotion  through
contributions  from  Government,  Public  Sector  Undertakings,  corporates  under  CSR,
institutions  and  individuals,  and  in  extending  targeted  assistance  to  sportspersons,
academies  and  the  Sports  Authority  of  India  under  the  Target  Olympic  Podium  Scheme
(TOPS).  However,  the  Committee  observes  that  allocations  under  the  Fund  have
witnessed  fluctuations,  with  BE  2025 – 26  of  ₹ 18.00  crore  being  reduced  to  ₹ 12.00  crore  at
RE  stage  and  BE  2026 – 27  approved  at  ₹ 15.00  crore  against  projections  of  ₹ 18.00  crore.
The  Committee  recommends  that  the  Ministry  should  evolve  a  more  practical  resource
mobilization  strategy,  including  advance  engagement  with  CSR  contributors  and  multi-
year  commitment  frameworks,  so  as  to  reduce  fluctuations  in  allocations  and  ensure
continuity of high-performance preparation of athletes.
(Para 6.3.7)
59. The  Committee  observes  that  Government  contribution  to  the  NSDF  is  linked  to  25  per
cent  of  funds  received  from  PSUs  and  corporates  under  CSR,  and  that  the  budgetary
reductions  at  RE  stage  is  attributable  to  lower  CSR  receipts  during  the  year.  The
Committee  recommends  that  the  Ministry  should  explore  diversification  of  funding
sources,  including  greater  outreach  to  private  foundations,  philanthropic  institutions  and
high  net-worth  individuals,  and  consider  instituting  incentive  mechanisms  for  long-term
endowments  so  as  to  augment  the  corpus  and  enhance  financial  sustainability  of  the
Fund.
(Para 6.3.8)
60. The  Committee  notes  that  substantial  block  grants  amounting  to  ₹ 58.94  crore  have  been
released  during  the  last  three  financial  years  to  SAI  under  TOPS  for  training,
international  exposure,  sports  science  support  and  procurement  of  high-performance
equipment,  thereby  benefiting  elite  athletes  across  disciplines.  In  order  to  further
strengthen  transparency,  the  Committee  recommends  that  the  Ministry  should  develop
institute  a  structured  impact  assessment  mechanism  with  performance  indicators,
periodic  public  disclosure  of  utilization  details,  and  measurable  outcome  benchmarks
linked  to  international  results,  so  as  to  ensure  optimal  utilization  of  NSDF  resources  and
greater accountability in sports promotion activities.

106
(Para 6.3.9)
61. The  Committee  observes  that  while  the  allocation  under  the  Scheme  “ Assistance  to
National  Sports  Federations  (NSFs) ”  has  increased  from  ₹ 400.00  crore  in  BE  2025 – 26  to
₹ 425.00  crore  in  BE  2026 – 27,  the  actual  expenditure  in  previous  years  ₹ 272.86  crore  in
2022 – 23,  ₹ 321.58  crore  in  2023 – 24  and  ₹ 256.05  crore  in  2024 – 25  indicates  persistent
under-utilisation  of  funds.  At  the  same  time,  the  projections  for  2026 – 27  ( ₹ 626.21  crore)
are  substantially  higher  than  the  Budget  Estimates,  reflecting  a  resource  gap  in  the
context  of  preparations  for  major  international  sporting  events  such  as  the  Asian  Games
2026,  Para  Asian  Games  2026  and  Commonwealth  Games  2026,  as  well  as  proposed
hosting  commitments.  The  Committee,  therefore,  recommends  that  a  performance-linked
disbursement  framework  with  quarterly  utilisation  reviews  and  outcome-based
benchmarks  should  be  put  in  place  for  National  Sports  Federations,  so  as  to  ensure  timely
fund  absorption,  improved  accountability,  and  optimal  utilisation  of  enhanced  allocations
towards sports development and international event preparedness.
(Para 6.4.4)
62. The  Committee  notes  that  the  Scheme  of  Human  Resource  Development  in  Sports
(HRDS)  is  important  for  strengthening  the  technical  and  professional  ecosystem  of  sports
through  capacity  building  of  coaches,  sports  scientists,  technical  officials  and  support
personnel.  While  the  BE  for  2026 – 27  has  been  enhanced  to  ₹ 5.00  crore  from  ₹ 4.00  crore
in  BE  2025 – 26  ( ₹ 2.00  crore  at  RE  stage),  the  pattern  of  utilisation  is  not  encouraging,  due
to  fewer  proposals  which  indicates  structural  constraints  in  outreach  and  absorption
capacity.  The  Committee  also  notes  that  despite  the  inclusion  of  new  components  such  as
support  for  publications,  e-publications  and  sports-related  digital  applications,  the
projected  requirement  of  ₹ 10.00  crore  has  not  been  fully  matched  in  the  Budget  Estimates
for  FY  2026-27.  The  Committee,  therefore,  recommends  that  the  Ministry  should  project
its  demand  for  higher  allocations  at  the  RE  Stage.  Besides,  the  Ministry  should  undertake
proactive  awareness  and  outreach  measures  in  coordination  with  National  Sports
Federations,  Sports  Universities  and  training  institutions  to  increase  the  number  and
quality  of  proposals  under  the  Scheme,  including  measures  to  ensure  optimal  utilisation
of funds to strengthen of the human resource base in sports.
(Para 6.5.6)
63. The  Committee  notes  with  concern  the  significant  fluctuations  in  budgetary  allocations
under  the  National  Centre  of  Sports  Sciences  &  Research  (NCSSR),  wherein  the  Budget
Estimate  of  ₹ 10.00  crore  for  2025 – 26  was  substantially  enhanced  to  ₹ 78.64  crore  at  the
Revised  Estimate  stage,  despite  actual  expenditure  being  ₹ 5.00  crore  in  FY  2024 – 25.
While  such  an  increase  may  reflect  major  capital  or  project-based  interventions  aimed  at
strengthening  sports  science  infrastructure,  the  sharp  decline  to  ₹ 15.00  crore  in  BE
2026 – 27  against  projections  of  ₹ 27.50  crore  and  an  80.93  per  cent  decrease  over  RE
indicates  the  absence  of  a  stable  funding  framework.  Given  the  critical  importance  of
scientific  support,  injury  management,  nutrition,  psychology  and  performance  analytics
in  achieving  international  competitiveness,  the  Committee  recommends  that  the  Ministry
should  formulate  outcome-oriented  financial  plan  for  NCSSR  with  clearly  defined

107
research  priorities,  institutional  partnerships  and  performance  indicators,  so  as  to  ensure
consistency in funding, and constant integration of sports science into athlete preparation.
(Para 6.6.4)
64. The  Committee  appreciates  that  Khelo  India,  as  the  flagship  programme  of  the  Ministry,
continues  to  command  the  largest  share  of  resources  and  has  made  notable  progress  in
grassroots  sports  development  through  the  approval  of  344  infrastructure  projects,
notification  of  1067  Khelo  India  Centres  (KICs),  35  Khelo  India  State  Centres  of
Excellence  (KISCEs),  accreditation  of  322  academies,  and  identification  of  2905  Khelo
India  Athletes.  However,  the  Committee  observes  a  consistent  gap  between  Budget
Estimates  and  actual  expenditure  during  the  last  three  financial  years,  with  actual
expenditure  in  2024 – 25  at  ₹ 620.75  crore  against  BE  of  ₹ 900  crore  and  RE  of  ₹ 746.54
crore,  and  similar  moderation  visible  in  2025 – 26  and  2026 – 27  allocations  vis-à-vis
utilisation  pattern.  The  Committee,  therefore,  is  of  the  view  that  the  advance  planning
and  phased  fund  release  mechanisms  should  be  adopted  in  consultation  with  States/UTs
to  minimize  surrender  of  funds  and  ensure  timely  execution  of  sanctioned  infrastructure
and competition-related activities.
(Para 6.7.15)
65. The  Committee  further  observes  that  while  55  infrastructure  projects  amounting  to
₹ 679.46  crore  were  approved  during  the  last  three  years,  only  17  projects  ( ₹ 384.97  crore)
have  been  completed,  indicating  implementation  bottlenecks.  Given  that  the  Scheme  is
demand-driven  and  funds  are  released  scheme-wise  rather  than  state-wise,  disparities  in
project  readiness  may  affect  uniform  sports  development.  The  Committee  recommends
that  a  project  monitoring  dashboard  with  tracking  and  third-party  technical  audits
support  to  State  implementing  agencies  be  developed  to  expedite  completion  of
infrastructure projects and ensure optimal utilization of investments.
(Para 6.7.16)
66. The  Committee  appreciates  the  comprehensive  athlete  support  framework  under  the
Khelo  India  Talent  Identification  &  Development  (KITD)  vertical,  including  financial
assistance  of  ₹ 6.28  lakh  per  annum  per  athlete  and  expansion  of  multiple  editions  of  the
Khelo  India  Games  with  participation  of  over  61,000  athletes.  However,  to  ensure
constant  high  performance  and  measurable  outcomes,  the  Committee  recommends  that
periodic  performance  evaluation  of  Khelo  India  Athletes  and  accredited  academies  be
undertaken  through  clearly  defined  benchmarks  linked  to  national  and  international
medal  prospects,  with  better  coordination  between  Khelo  India  Centres,  State  Centres  of
Excellence and National Coaching Camps.
(Para 6.7.17)
67.
The  Committee  also  notes  the  integration  of  allied  initiatives  such  as  women ’s  leagues
across  34  disciplines,  promotion  of  indigenous  sports,  and  alignment  with  mass
participation  movements  like  Fit  India.  While  these  initiatives  contribute  to  social
inclusion,  gender  equity  and  community  engagement,  the  Committee  recommends  that  an
impact  assessment  framework  taking  into  account  participation  metrics,  and  socio-

108
economic  outreach  indicators  should  be  developed,  so  as  to  evaluate  long-term  outcomes
and monitor future budgetary allocations should be developed under the Scheme.
(Para 6.7.18)
68. The  Committee  notes  that  the  Scheme  for  Enhancement  of  Sports  Facility  in  Jammu  &
Kashmir  has  exhibited  considerable  budgetary  fluctuation,  with  no  expenditure  incurred
in  FY  2024 – 25.  On  the  other  hand,  the  Budget  Estimate  of  ₹ 20.00  crore  for  FY  2025 – 26
reduced  to  ₹ 2.00  crore  only  at  the  Revised  Estimate  stage,  and  the  allocation  for  FY
2026 – 27  fixed  at  ₹ 5.00  crore  against  projections  of  ₹ 8.00  crore.  Such  wide  variations  may
delay  in  project  finalization  and  implementation,  or  procedural  constraints  in  fund
release.  Considering  the  strategic  importance  of  strengthening  sports  infrastructure  in
Jammu  &  Kashmir  for  youth  engagement  and  integration  of  regional  talent  into  national
competitions,  the  Committee  recommends  that  a  time-bound,  project-specific
implementation  roadmap  with  clear  milestones,  streamlined  approval  processes  and
quarterly  monitoring  mechanisms  should  be  prepared,  so  as  to  ensure  timely  execution  of
sanctioned  works  and  avoid  cost  overruns  to  achieve  intended  sporting  outcomes  in  the
region.
(Para 6.8.3)
69. General  industrial  zones  often  lack  the  required  testing  labs  required  for  sports
equipment.  The  Committee  is  of  the  view  that  manufacturing  centres  in  exiting  industrial
hubs,  already  equipped  with  common  facilities  for  carbon  fiber  molding,  wood  seasoning,
and  synthetic  leather  processing  may  be  developed  for  successful  implementation  of
sports goods manufacturing schools. 
(Para 6.9.6)
70. The  Committee  may  recommend  that  the  Department  of  Sports  ensure  timely  declaration
of  National  Sports  Awards  every  year  through  a  fixed  annual  calendar.  Further,  the
selection  criteria,  evaluation  parameters  and  marks  awarded  by  the  selection  committee
may  be  placed  in  the  public  domain  after  the  awards  are  announced.  This  would  enhance
transparency,  credibility  and  public  confidence  in  the  award  selection  process  while
ensuring that deserving sportspersons and coaches are recognized without undue delay.
(Para 6.10.3)
71. The  Committee  may  also  recommend  that  the  Government  periodically  revise  the
quantum  of  cash  incentives  and  pension  provided  under  the  Scheme  of  Cash  Incentive  to
Medal  Winners  and  the  Sports  Fund  for  Pension  to  Meritorious  Sportspersons,  taking
into  account  inflation  and  rising  training  costs.  The  scope  of  the  pension  scheme  may  also
be  expanded  to  cover  a  larger  number  of  deserving  retired  sportspersons,  particularly
those  from  less-popular  sports  disciplines,  to  ensure  financial  security  and  encourage
greater participation in sports across the country.
(Para 6.10.4)

109
72. The  Committee  notes  that  the  Scheme  of  Cash  Incentive  to  Medal  Winners  in
International  Sports  Events  and  their  Coaches  has  played  a  vital  role  in  rewarding
excellence  and  motivating  sportspersons  to  achieve  higher  standards  at  global  platforms.
However,  in  view  of  the  evolving  competitive  landscape  and  rising  performance
benchmarks,  the  Committee  recommends  that  a  comprehensive  review  of  the  incentive
structure  should  be  undertaken  to  ensure  timely  disbursement,  rationalization  of  award
slabs  in  line  with  inflation  and  international  standards,  and  greater  transparency  in
eligibility  criteria,  particularly  with  regard  to  coaches  and  support  staff  who  contribute
significantly to medal-winning performances.
(Para 6.13.5)
73. The  Committee  further  observes  that  while  the  National  Sports  Awards  continue  to
recognize  outstanding  sportspersons  and  coaches  under  various  categories,  fluctuations  in
the  number  of  awardees  and  the  discontinuation  or  restructuring  of  certain  awards  call
for  greater  clarity  and  consistency.  The  Committee  recommends  that  the  Ministry
establish  a  stable,  well-publicized  selection  calendar  with  clearly  defined  evaluation
parameters,  digital  nomination  processes,  and  wider  stakeholder  consultation  so  as  to
preserve the prestige of the Awards and enhance their credibility and outreach.
(Para 6.13.6)
74. With  regard  to  the  Scheme  of  Sports  Fund  for  Pension  to  Meritorious  Sportspersons,  the
Committee  notes  a  declining  trend  in  allocation,  and  number  of  beneficiaries  over  the  last
five  years  despite  the  Scheme ’s  objective  of  providing  assured  post-retirement  financial
security.  The  Committee  recommends  to  review  eligibility  norms  and  pension  rates
periodically,  expand  the  beneficiary  base  where  warranted,  and  ensure  automatic  revision
mechanisms  linked  to  cost-of-living  criteria  so  that  former  sportspersons  who  have
brought laurels to the nation are not left in financial distress.
(Para 6.13.7)
75. The  Committee  also  notes  that  under  the  Pandit  Deendayal  Upadhyay  National  Welfare
Program  for  Sportspersons,  allocations  have  remained  constant  at  ₹ 2.00  crore  annually,
while  expenditure  has  shown  a  gradual  decline  in  recent  years.  Considering  that  the
Scheme  is  intended  to  provide  urgent  financial  assistance  for  medical  treatment,  injuries
and  other  exigencies  of  sports  persons/  their  family  members,  the  Committee  recommends
that  to  strengthen  awareness  among  eligible  beneficiaries,  simplify  application  procedures
through  an  online  portal,  and  create  a  fast-track  mechanism  for  medical  emergencies  to
ensure  that  deserving  sportspersons  receive  timely  support.  Further,  the  Committee  also
recommends  that  necessary  steps  should  be  taken  for  continuation  of  this  scheme  during
the next Financial Cycle ( i.e.  2026-2031)
(Para 6.13.8)

110
76. The  Committee  further  observes  that  the  various  talent  development  and  training
schemes  implemented  through  SAI  institutions — including  National  Centres  of  Excellence
(NCOEs),  SAI  Training  Centres  (STCs),  NSTC  and  Indigenous  Games  initiatives — have
detailed  induction,  retention  and  monitoring  criteria  based  on  scientific  assessment  and
performance  benchmarks.  While  these  frameworks  are  comprehensive,  the  Committee
recommends  that  a  unified  digital  athlete  management  system  be  developed  to  integrate
performance  data,  scientific  assessments,  training  diaries,  medical  records  and  academic
support  details  across  all  centres , thereby  enabling  real-time  monitoring,  minimizing
duplication,  preventing  age  fraud  and  doping-related  irregularities,  and  ensuring  a
transparent and merit-based development from grassroots to elite levels.
(Para 6.13.9)
77. The  Committee  notes  that  the  Autonomous  Bodies  under  the  Department  of
Sports — namely  the  Sports  Authority  of  India  (SAI),  National  Anti-Doping
Agency  (NADA),  National  Dope  Testing  Laboratory  (NDTL),  National  Sports
University  (NSU)  and  Lakshmibai  National  Institute  of  Physical  Education
(LNIPE) — play  a  pivotal  role  in  strengthening  training  infrastructure,  anti-
doping  mechanisms,  sports  education  and  scientific  support  systems.  While
the  overall  allocation  under  Other  Central  Sector  Expenditure  –  Autonomous
Bodies  has  increased  from  ₹ 1,603.73  crore  at  RE  2025 – 26  to  ₹ 1,777.90  crore  at
BE  2026 – 27,  there  is  a  considerable  variations  and  fluctuations  in  fund
allocations  between  Autonomous  Bodies  over  the  years.  The  Committee
recommends  that  a  performance-based  funding  framework  may  be  adopted
for  Autonomous  Bodies,  with  a  focus  to  measurable  outcomes  such  as  medal
performance,  research  output,  anti-doping  compliance  standards  and  athlete
outreach parameters.
(Para 7.3.5)
78. The  Committee  observes  variations  in  fund  allocations  to  a  few  institutions,
particularly  the  National  Sports  University  and  the  National  Dope  Testing
Laboratory,  with  significant  differences  between  Budget  Estimates  and
Revised  Estimates  in  successive  years.  Such  variations  may  affect  planning,
faculty  recruitment,  research  continuity  and  laboratory  accreditation
standards.  The  Committee,  therefore,  recommends  that  annual  budgetary
provisions  be  aligned  with  approved  long-term  development  plans  of  these
institutions,  with  funds  provisions  for  committed  liabilities  and  capital
projects, so as to ensure stability of such institutions.
(Para 7.3.6)
79. The  Committee  also  notes  the  central  role  of  the  Sports  Authority  of  India,
which  continues  to  receive  the  largest  share  of  funding  among  Autonomous
Bodies,  alongside  critical  institutions  such  as  NADA,  NDTL  etc.  In  view  of  the
increasing  emphasis  on  clean  and  scientific  training  of  sports,  the  Committee
recommends  that  greater  institutional  coordination  among  SAI,  NADA,
NDTL,  NSU  and  LNIPE  should  be  developed  through  a  inter-institutional

111
coordination  mechanism  for  data  sharing,  research  collaboration,  sports
science  integration  and  athlete  monitoring,  thereby  optimizing  resource
utilization and enhancing sports performance ecosystem of the country.
(Para7.3.7)
80. The  Committee  notes  that  the  Sports  Authority  of  India  (SAI)  continues  to  play  a  central
role  in  achieving  sporting  excellence  through  its  multi-tiered  talent  identification  and
training  framework  comprising  National  Centres  of  Excellence  (NCOEs),  SAI  Training
Centres  (STCs),  Extension  Centres  and  NSTC  schemes.  In  view  of  the  enhanced
allocation  of  ₹ 917.38  crore  in  BE  2026 – 27  as  compared  to  ₹ 880  crore  at  RE  2025 – 26,  the
Committee  recommends  that  SAI  should  formulate  performance  based  such  as  medal
conversion  rates,  athlete  progression  from  STCs  to  NCOEs,  and  other  performance
benchmarks  to  financial  allocations,  so  as  to  ensure  optimal  utilization  of  enhanced
budgetary support.
(Para 7.2.18)
81. The  Committee  observes  that  while  SAI  has  expanded  its  footprint  across  10  Regional
Centres  and  2  Academic  Institutions  with  187  operational  units  nationwide,  disparities  in
infrastructure  development  and  utilization  and  athlete  density  across  regions  remain  as
bottlenecks  for  balanced  sports  development.  The  Committee  recommends  that  a  region-
wise  capacity  and  performance  audit  be  undertaken  to  rationalize  disciplines  based  on
local  strengths,  optimize  coach  deployment,  and  ensure  equitable  access  to  high-quality
training facilities, particularly in tribal, rural and remote areas.
(Para 7.2.19)
82. The  Committee  appreciates  the  structured  talent  scouting  mechanism  under  Khelo  India,
TOPS  Development  Group  and  Long-Term  Athlete  Development  (LTAD)  framework,
supported  by  scientific  monitoring  and  multidisciplinary  teams.  However,  to  strengthen
transparency  and  long-term  athlete  tracking,  the  Committee  recommends  development  of
an  integrated  digital  athlete  performance  management  system  across  all  SAI  schemes,
taking  into  account  data  on  training  load,  injury  management,  competition  results  etc. ,
thereby  enabling  practical  decision-making  for  overall  development  of  Sports  in  the
country.
(Para 7.2.20)
83. The  Committee  takes  note  of  the  recruitment  of  coaches  under  regular  and  contractual
categories  and  recognizes  the  importance  of  qualified  human  resources  in  achieving  high
performance  standards.  The  Committee,  therefore,  recommends  to  expedite  the  filling  up
of  sanctioned  vacancies  through  a  time-bound  recruitment  calendar,  thereby  enhancing
continuous  professional  development  of  coaches  through  international  certifications  and
exposure.
(Para 7.2.21)

112
84. The  Committee  acknowledges  the  measures  adopted  by  SAI  to  ensure  safety  and  welfare
of  athletes,  including  constitution  of  Internal  Complaints  Committees,  24×7  toll-free
helplines  and  hostel  security  arrangements.  While  these  initiatives  are  commendable,  the
Committee  is  of  the  view  that  periodic  independent  safety  audits  of  SAI  centres ,
mandatory  gender-sensitization  and  safeguarding  workshops  for  all  staff  and  trainees,
should  be  considered,  so  as  to  create  a  secure  and  sporting  environment  across  all  SAI
establishments.
(Para 7.2.22)
85. The  Committee  notes  that  the  Lakshmibai  National  Institute  of  Physical  Education
(LNIPE),  Gwalior,  is  dedicated  to  higher  education,  research  and  specialization  in
physical  education  and  sports  sciences  and  plays  a  crucial  role  in  strengthening  the
academic  and  professional  foundation  of  the  sports  ecosystem  in  the  country.  While  the
enhanced  allocation  of  ₹ 87.50  crore  in  BE  2026 – 27  over  ₹ 79.96  crore  at  RE  2025 – 26  is
primarily  attributable  to  salary  revisions,  pension  liabilities,  filling  up  of  posts  and
anticipated  construction  activities  at  both  Gwalior  and  NERC  campuses,  the  Committee
is  of  the  view  that  a  comprehensive  academic  and  infrastructure  development  plan  should
be  prepared  with  clearly  defined  research  priorities,  faculty  recruitment  plans  etc.  so  as  to
ensure  that  higher  allocations  translates  into  tangible  improvements  in  teaching  quality,
research output and institutional excellence in physical education and sports sciences.
(Para 7.3.3)
86. The  Committee  notes  with  appreciation  that  the  Fit  India  Movement  has  evolved  into  a
nationwide  behavioural  transformation  initiative,  with  extensive  outreach  through
programmes  such  as  Sundays  on  Cycle,  Fit  India  Freedom  Run,  National  Sports  Day
celebrations,  International  Yoga  Day  activities,  Fit  India  Carnivals,  Cycling  Clubs  and
the  Fit  India  Mobile  App.  The  large-scale  participation  across  districts,  educational
institutions  and  rural  areas  reflects  commendable  public  engagement  and  policy
convergence.  However,  in  order  to  ensure  sustained  impact  beyond  event-based
mobilization,  the  Committee  recommends  that  a  systematic  monitoring  and  evaluation
framework  with  outcome  indicators  such  as  measurable  improvements  in  physical
activity  levels  in  schools  and  communities,  integration  of  physical  education  in  curricula,
utilisation  metrics  of  Fit  India  Zones  and  Cycling  Clubs,  and  behavioral  change  surveys
may  be  developed  so  that  the  Movement  transitions  from  mass  participation  campaigns  to
a fitness ecosystem with long-term public health and youth development goals.
(Para 7.4.3)
87. The  Committee  notes  with  satisfaction  that  the  National  Dope  Testing  Laboratory
(NDTL)  has  secured  continued  accreditation  from  WADA  and  NABL  under  ISO
17025:2017,  established  a  WADA-approved  Athlete  Passport  Management  Unit,  and
strengthened  its  Quality  Management  Systems,  research  collaborations  and

113
infrastructure  in  accordance  with  international  standards.  These  developments  signify
India ’s  growing  credibility  in  the  global  anti-doping  framework.  However,  considering
the  dynamic  nature  of  doping  methodologies  and  the  need  for  constant  technological
upgradation , the  Committee  recommends  that  the  Ministry  should  ensure  assured  and
dedicated  financial  support  for  periodic  modernization  of  analytical  equipment,
expansion  of  research  capabilities,  and  continuous  capacity  building  of  scientific
personnel,  so  that  NDTL  remains  at  par  with  leading  WADA-accredited  laboratories
worldwide.
(Para 7.5.5)
88. The  Committee  notes  a  sharp  decline  in  internal  revenue  generation  from  2024 – 25
onwards  due  to  the  waiver  of  Dope  Control  Testing  Fee  for  NADA,  which  constitutes  the
majority  of  sample  inflow.  While  the  waiver  may  serve  broader  policy  objectives,  the
resultant  reduction  in  institutional  revenue  generation  could  impact  long-term
operational  sustainability  and  research  activities.  The  Committee,  therefore,  recommends
that  a  calibrated  funding  mechanism — either  through  enhanced  budgetary  support  or
diversification  of  testing  services  including  regional  collaborations  and  specialised
research  assignments  should  be  developed  to  compensate  for  the  revenue  shortfall  and
ensure that the Laboratory ’s financial stability is not compromised.
(Para7.5.6)
89. The  Committee  notes  that  the  National  Anti-Doping  Agency  (NADA)  plays  a  crucial  role
in  maintaining  the  integrity  of  Indian  sports  through  testing,  results  management,
regulatory  enforcement  and  extensive  education  and  awareness  generation  initiatives.
While  the  Agency  has  instituted  robust  mechanisms  in  compliance  with  the  World  Anti-
Doping  Code,  including  trained  Doping  Control  Officers,  strict  chain-of-custody
procedures  and  dissemination  of  educational  materials  in  regional  languages,  the  data  on
Anti-Doping  Rule  Violations  (ADRVs)  indicates  fluctuating  trends  and  continuous
violations  in  recent  years.  The  Committee,  therefore,  recommends  that  NADA  should
take  targeted  initiatives,  sport-specific  and  grassroots-level  preventive  educational
programmes,  particularly  in  high-incidence  disciplines  and  vulnerable  regions,  with  a
focus  on  supplement  awareness,  inadvertent  doping  risks  and  planned  engagement
through federations and training academies. 
(Para 7.6.8)
90. The  Committee  notes  that  the  Budget  Estimates  for  both  NADA  and  the  National  Dope
Testing  Laboratory  (NDTL)  for  FY  2026 – 27  has  reduced  compared  to  the  Revised
Estimates  of  FY  2025 – 26,  even  though  anti-doping  monitoring  responsibilities  and
international  compliance  obligations  continue  to  expand  as  cases  of  Anti-Doping  Rule
violation  cases  continue  to  occur  as  evident  from  Table  no.  36.  Although  the  allocation  is
stated  to  be  adequate  at  the  BE  stage,  continues  testing,  technological  upgradation  and

114
expanded  outreach  require  stable  financial  support.  The  Committee  recommends  that  the
Ministry  closely  monitor  fund  utilisation  and  testing  coverage  during  the  financial  year
should  be  monitored  and  ensure  timely  augmentation  of  funds  at  the  Revised  Estimates
stage,  if  warranted,  so  that  financial  constraints  do  not  adversely  impact  testing  frequency
or international compliance commitments.
(Para 7.6.9)
91. The  Committee  notes  that  the  National  Sports  University  (NSU),  envisioned  as  a  premier
institution  for  sports  education,  research  and  training,  has  made  progress  in  launching
specialised  undergraduate,  postgraduate  and  doctoral  programmes  in  Sports  Coaching,
Sports  Sciences,  Sports  Psychology  and  Applied  Sports  Nutrition.  The  University ’s
collaborations  with  reputed  international  universities  and  national  research  institutions,
along  with  its  focus  on  innovation  and  integration  of  laboratory  research  into  real-time
sports  settings,  reflect  its  potential  to  emerge  as  a  centre  of  excellence.  However,  the
Committee  notes  that  the  University  continues  to  function  from  a  temporary  campus  at
Khuman  Lampak  Sports  Complex,  Imphal.  The  Committee,  therefore,  recommends  that
priority  to  the  expeditious  completion  and  operationalisation  of  the  permanent  campus
with  world-class  academic,  research  and  residential  infrastructure  should  be  accorded,  so
that  the  University  may  fully  realise  its  mandate  as  a  national  training  centre  and  global
hub for sports education and research.
(Para 7.7.5)
92. The  Committee  further  notes  that  while  NSU  has  developed  modern  laboratories,  sports
infrastructure  and  industry  partnerships,  the  intake  capacity  across  programmes  remains
relatively  modest  considering  the  national  demand  for  qualified  sports  scientists,  coaches
and  support  professionals.  In  order  to  strengthen  India ’s  sports  ecosystem  move  in  a
fruitful  manner,  the  Committee  recommends  that  a  phased  expansion  plan  for  increasing
student  intake,  diversifying  specialised  disciplines,  and  enhancing  faculty  strength,
alongside  sustainable  Operation  &  Maintenance  mechanisms  should  be  formulated  for
better  development  of  Sports  ecosystem  in  the  country.  Greater  integration  with  National
Centres  of  Excellence,  Khelo  India  Centres  and  sports  federations  may  also  be
institutionalised  to  ensure  that  academic  output  directly  contributes  to  athlete
performance enhancement and capacity building across the country.
(Para 7.7.6)
93. The  Committee  notes  that  the  overall  representation  of  Women  and  other  categories  in
the  Main  Secretariat  and  in  key  technical  and  scientific  institutions  such  as  NADA,  NDTL
and  NSU  including  SAI  is  not  encouraging.  The  Committee,  therefore,  recommends  that  a
comprehensive  recruitment  policy  may  be  formulated  with  measurable  targets  and
periodic  review  mechanisms  to  enhance  representation  of  women  and  other  categories  in
various fields required for development of Sports ecosystem in the country.
(Para 8.6)

115
94. The  Committee  further  observes  variations  in  representation  of  SC,  ST,  OBC  and
Persons  with  Disabilities  (PWD)  across  organisations , with  comparatively  low  PWD
representation  and  certain  shortfalls  in  reserved  categories  in  specific  bodies.  While
reservation  policies  are  stated  to  be  followed  as  per  Government  norms,  the  Committee
recommends  that  a  detailed  reservation  roster  audit  be  undertaken  across  all  institutions
under  the  Department  of  Sports  to  identify  backlog  vacancies,  if  any,  and  to  initiate
special  recruitment  drives  to  ensure  full  compliance  with  statutory  reservation  provisions
and inclusive workforce participation.
(Para 8.7)
95. The  Committee  notes  the  considerable  reliance  on  contractual  appointments  over  the  past
five  years,  particularly  in  SAI  and  NADA,  where  contractual  engagement  appears
significantly  higher  than  permanent  recruitment.  While  contractual  hiring  may  provide
operational  flexibility,  continuous  dependence  on  such  arrangements  may  affect
institutional  memory,  continuity  of  programmes  and  accountability  structures.  The
Committee  recommends  that  a  manpower  rationalisation  exercise  should  be  undertaken
to  assess  core  versus  project-based  requirements  and  progressively  regularise  essential
positions  through  sanctioned  permanent  posts,  thereby  ensuring  long-term  organisational
stability.
(Para 8.8)
96. The  Committee  also  observes  that  permanent  recruitment  in  several  institutions  such  as
NSU,  NDTL  and  LNIPE  has  been  limited  in  recent  years,  which  may  constrain  academic
expansion,  research  output  and  technical  efficiency.  The  Committee  recommends  that
recruitment  processes  be  streamlined  and  expedited  in  coordination  with  the  competent
authorities so that sanctioned posts are filled in a time-bound manner.
(Para 8.9)
97. The  Committee  notes  about  the  vacancy  position  at  LNIPE,  NERC,  Guwahati , including
the  vacant  Professor  post  and  two  Assistant  Professor  posts . Given  the  critical  role  of
faculty  in  maintaining  academic  excellence,  research  supervision  and  curriculum
development,  the  Committee  recommends  that  immediate  steps  be  taken  to  fill  these
vacancies  and  to  periodically  review  sanctioned  strength  in  light  of  expanding  academic
programmes and student intake.
(Para 8.10)
98. The  Committee  recommends  that  a  centralised  Human  Resource  Monitoring  and
Planning  Framework  within  the  Department  of  Sports  may  be  established,  incorporating
sanctioned  strength,  persons  in  position,  vacancies,  category-wise  representation,
contractual  engagements  and  recruitment  timelines  across  all  subordinate  bodies.  Such  a
structured  monitoring  mechanism  would  enhance  transparency,  facilitate  evidence-based

116
workforce  planning  and  ensure  optimal  deployment  of  human  resources  in  furtherance  of
the Department ’s expanding mandate and long-term strategic objectives.
(Para 8.11)
